# PAC-Bayesian Bounds for Learning Partially Observed Stochastic Linear Time-Invariant State-Space Systems with Inputs and Sub-Gaussian Noise

Mihaly Petreczky

CRIStAL, Centrale Lille, Universite de Lille, France, ´ mihaly.petreczky@centralelille.fr

Mohamad Al Ahdab

Department of Electronic Systems, Aalborg University, Denmark, maah@es.aau.dk

John Leth

Department of Electronic Systems, Aalborg University, Denmark, jjl@es.aau.dk

## Abstract

In this paper we derive a Probably Approximately Correct (PAC)-Bayesian error bound for partially observed linear time-invariant (LTI) stochastic dynamical systems in state-space form with inputs. Such bounds are widespread in machine learning, and they are useful for characterizing the predictive power of models learned from finitely many data points. The bound derived in this paper relates the expectation of prediction errors with the prediction error generated by the model on the data used for learning. In addition, we show that it can also be used to derive bounds for the parameter estimation error. In turn, this allows us to provide finite-sample error bounds for the prediction error and parameter estimation error for a wide class of system identification algorithms. Furthermore, as LTI systems are a sub-class of recurrent neural networks (RNNs), these error bounds could be a first step towards PAC-Bayesian bounds for RNNs.

## 1 Introduction

PAC and PAC-Bayesian bounds are fundamental tools for analyzing learning algorithms. They provide generalization guarantees that relate the true error (performance on unseen data) to the empirical error (performance on training data), by bounding the generalization gap, the difference between true and empirical error, in a way that is independent of the specific learning algorithm. In this framework, learning is viewed as selecting a model from a data-dependent posterior distribution over hypotheses, which is a modification of a prior based on the observed data. Different learning algorithms correspond to different choices of priors, posteriors, and selection mechanisms (e.g., sampling, averaging, or maximizing the posterior). PAC-Bayesian bounds then provide a uniform upper bound on the average generalization gap with respect to any posterior, thus offering generalization guarantees for a broad class of learning and model selection methods.

This generality makes PAC-Bayesian approaches especially useful for analyzing complex models, including neural networks, where they can yield non-vacuous generalization bounds [7]. Although PAC and PAC-Bayesian theory is well developed for i.i.d. settings [36, 2, 17], there are comparatively few results for dynamical systems.

Contribution: In this paper, we derive PAC-Bayesian error bounds for learning discrete-time stochastic LTI state-space systems with sub-Gaussian noise and partial observations (the full states is not observed). Following standard practice in system identification, we view LTI systems as predictors that generate predictions for the current output based on past inputs and outputs. Our main contribution is a PAC-Bayesian bound showing that, with high probability (at least $\bar { 1 } - \delta )$ , for any posterior distribution over predictors, the average generalization gap is $O \big ( \frac { ( \ln N ) ^ { r } } { \sqrt { N } } \big )$ , where N is the sample size, $r = 0$ for bounded noise, $r = 1$ for Lipschitz loss, and $r = 3 / 2$ for quadratic loss. The bound depends on the Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence between prior and posterior distributions and on $\bar { \ln ( 1 / \delta ) }$ . We subsequently derive generalization gap and parameter estimation error bounds for concrete learning algorithms $( \mathrm { e . g . }$ , maximum likelihood, regularized prediction-error methods (PEM), and Bayesian methods).

Related work: PAC and PAC-Bayesian bounds for autoregressive models with bounded signals or Lipschitz loss have been studied in [5, 44, 3, 1, 27, 29]. In contrast, our results address state-space models with partially observed states and with inputs, which are more general than autoregressive models, and we allow for unbounded (sub-Gaussian) noise, and handle quadratic loss. We also build on techniques from [3, 1].

In contrast to the literature on finite-sample parameter estimation bounds for specific least-squares based learning algorithms for LTI systems, e.g., [38, 37, 32, 24, 14, 19, 42, 20, 35], this papers presents bounds on both the parameter estimation and the generalization gap for a wide-range of algorithms, see Remark D.12, Appendix D.5 for a detailed comparison.

PAC bounds for recurrent neural networks, of which LTI state-space representations are a subclass, have been developed using VC dimension [23, 41], Rademacher complexity [23, 41, 6], and PAC-Bayesian approaches [7]. However, existing results assume noiseless models, fixed prediction horizons, i.i.d. training data samples, and bounded signals. In contrast, this paper considers noisy models, infinite-horizon prediction errors, a single observed time series, and unbounded signals with sub-Gaussian noise.

In [12], bounds based on Renyi divergence were proposed, but the resulting probabilistic guarantees´ scale as ${ \cal O } ( 1 / \delta )$ with the confidence level, rather than the standard $O ( \ln ( 1 / \delta ) )$ ), and it applies only to quadratic losses as opposed to general Lipschitz losses, and it provides parameter estimation bounds only in terms of the empirical error. In contrast, the bound of this paper depends on the confidence level $O ( \ln ( 1 / \delta ) )$ ) and it provides an absolute bound on the parameter estimation error. The works [11, 8] provide PAC-Bayesian generalization gap bounds for RNNs and LTI systems with bounded noise, respectively. In contrast, the present paper addresses unbounded (sub-Gaussian) noise and also derives parameter estimation error bounds. Even for bounded signals, our results refine the constants used in [11, 8] (see Remark D.11, Appendix D.5). PAC-Bayesian bounds for autonomous LTI systems without inputs were considered in [9] but those bounds did not converge to zero as $N \to \infty$

Paper Outline: We start by defining the problem formulation in Section 2, followed by the main results in Section 3 and discussion of the bounds in Section 4. The technical proofs are presented in the appendices.

## 2.2 Problem formulation

The problem formulation follows [11, 8, 12].

Let us fix stationary stochastic processes $\mathbf { y } ( t ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { \mathbf { y } } }$ , and $\mathbf { u } ( t ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { \mathbf { u } } }$ , that share a time axis $t \in \mathbb { Z } ,$ that is, for any $t \in \mathbb { Z } , \mathbf { y } ( t ) : \bar { \Omega } \to \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { \mathbf { y } } } ; \boldsymbol { \omega } \mapsto \mathbf { y } ( t ) ( \boldsymbol { \omega } )$ , and $\mathbf { u } ( t ) : \Omega \to \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { \mathbf { u } } } ; \omega \mapsto \mathbf { u } ( t ) ( \omega )$ are random vectors on $( \Omega , { \mathcal { F } } , { \dot { \mathbf { P } } } ) { \mathrm { ; } }$ y represents outputs (labels), and u represents inputs (features). The goal is to learn models from the finite sample $\mathcal { D } = \{ \mathbf { y } ( t ) ( \omega ) , \mathbf { u } ( t ) ( \omega ) \} _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } , \omega \in \Omega$ , such that the learned model predicts y from past measurements of $( \mathbf { y } , \mathbf { u } )$ sufficiently well.

Predictor class: To simplify notation, let us define a stochastic process $\mathbf { w } ( t ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { \mathbf { w } } }$ by

$\mathbf { w } ( t ) = \left[ \mathbf { y } ^ { T } ( t ) , \mathbf { u } ^ { T } ( t ) \right] ^ { T }$ when $n _ { \mathbf { w } } = n _ { \mathbf { y } } + n _ { \mathbf { u } }$ and both past y and u are used for prediction, $\mathbf { w } ( t ) = \mathbf { u } ( t )$ when $n _ { \mathbf { w } } = n _ { \mathbf { u } }$ , when only past u is used for prediction.

Note that with a trivial extension of the results of this paper one can define ${ \bf w } ( t )$ to consist of only some of the components of $\mathbf { y } ( t )$ , extending the results to not only prediction but also filtering.

Assumption 2.1 (Predictors). The class of predictors is the class of LTI systems parametrized by $\theta \in \Theta \bar { \subseteq } \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { \theta } }$ which generate predictions $\hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t | t _ { 0 } )$ of ${ \bf { \dot { y } } } ( t )$ based on $\{ \mathbf { w } ( s ) \} _ { s = t _ { ( } } ^ { t }$ t<sub>0</sub>

$$
\hat { \mathbf { x } } ( t + 1 ) = \hat { A } _ { \theta } \hat { \mathbf { x } } ( t ) + \hat { B } _ { \theta } \mathbf { w } ( t ) , \ \hat { \mathbf { x } } ( t _ { 0 } ) = 0 , \quad \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t | t _ { 0 } ) = \hat { C } _ { \theta } \hat { \mathbf { x } } ( t ) + \hat { D } _ { \theta } \mathbf { w } ( t ) , \quad t \geq t _ { 0 } ,\tag{1}
$$

where $\hat { A } _ { \theta } , \hat { B } _ { \theta } , \hat { C } _ { \theta } , \hat { D } _ { \theta }$ are $\hat { n } \times \hat { n } , \hat { n } \times n _ { \mathbf { w } } , n _ { \mathbf { y } } \times \hat { n }$ and $n _ { \mathbf { y } } \times n _ { \mathbf { w } }$ matrices respectively, they are are continuous in $\theta ,$ and $\hat { A } _ { \theta }$ is Schur (all its eigenvalues are inside the unit disk). Moreover, $i f \mathbf { w } ( t ) =$ $[ \mathbf { y } ^ { T } ( t ) \mathbf { u } ^ { T } ( t ) ] ^ { T }$ , then $\hat { D } _ { \theta } = [ 0 \hat { D } _ { \mathbf { u } , \theta } ]$ for some $n _ { \mathbf { y } } \times n _ { \mathbf { u } }$ matrix $\hat { D } _ { \mathbf { u } , \theta \cdot } ^ { \phantom { \dagger } } ^ { \phantom { \dagger } 1 }$ Moreover, there exist constants $M , C > 0 , \gamma \in ( 0 , 1 )$ such thatfor all $\theta \in \Theta , \| \hat { A } _ { \theta } ^ { k } \| _ { 2 } \leq M \gamma ^ { k }$ , ma $\mathrm { x } \{ \| \hat { B } _ { \theta } \| _ { 2 } , \| \hat { C } _ { \theta } \| _ { 2 } , \| \hat { D } _ { \theta } \| _ { 2 } \} \leq C$

Intuitively, our assumption expresses that the predictors are stable LTIs and we have a uniform upper bound on the matrices and on the spectral radius of $\hat { A } _ { \theta }$ . These assumptions are standard in system identification, see Remark A.3, Appendix A. Note that the predictors are started at the zero initial state at inital time t<sub>0</sub>. The latter represents the time instant at which the predictor was applied to generate predictions, or when learning started. Most of the time, we will set $t _ { 0 } = 0$ to simplify notation.

Data generator: Intuitively, we would like the data y to be generated by an LTI system

$$
\hat { \mathbf { s } } _ { g } ( t + 1 ) = A _ { 0 } \hat { \mathbf { s } } _ { g } ( t ) + B _ { 0 } \mathbf { u } ( t ) + K _ { 0 } \mathbf { e } ^ { s } ( t ) , \quad \mathbf { y } ( t ) = C _ { 0 } \hat { \mathbf { s } } _ { g } ( t ) + D _ { 0 } \mathbf { u } ( t ) + \mathbf { e } ^ { s } ( t ) , \quad t \in \mathbb { Z } ,\tag{2}
$$

where $A _ { 0 } , B _ { 0 } , K _ { 0 } , C _ { 0 } , D _ { 0 }$ are matrices of suitable dimension, $A _ { 0 }$ and $A _ { 0 } - K _ { 0 } C _ { 0 }$ are both Schur, and $\mathbf { e } ^ { s } ( t )$ is a zero-mean i.i.d. sub-Gaussian process. The Schur conditions ensure stationarity of x and that $\mathbf { e } ^ { s } ( t )$ is the innovation process (see [26, Chapter 4, p. 87]), i.e., the difference between ${ \bf y } ( t )$ and its best linear predictor based on past inputs and outputs. Thus, (2) is in steady-state Kalman filter form. Unlike predictors, the data generator (2) is assumed to operate in steady state, i.e., it has run for infinite time prior to prediction. This standard assumption in system identification $\left( \mathrm { e . g . , } \left[ 2 6 \right] \right)$ reflects that the physical system has been subject to noise and inputs long before learning begins. This subtle difference has significant theoretical implications.

Assuming the data is generated by a system of the same form as the predictors is a realizability assumption, as (2) yields the optimal predictor.

$$
\hat { \mathbf { s } } _ { g } ( t + 1 \mid t _ { 0 } ) = \hat { A } _ { 0 } \hat { \mathbf { s } } _ { g } ( t ) + \hat { B } _ { 0 } \mathbf { w } ( t ) , \hat { \mathbf { s } } _ { g } ( t _ { 0 } | t _ { 0 } ) = 0 , \hat { \mathbf { y } } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) = \hat { C } _ { 0 } \hat { \mathbf { s } } _ { g } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) + \hat { D } _ { 0 } \mathbf { w } ( t ) ,\tag{3}
$$

where $t \ge t _ { 0 } , \hat { A } _ { 0 } = A _ { 0 } , \hat { B } _ { 0 } = B _ { 0 } , \hat { C } _ { 0 } = C _ { 0 } , \hat { D } _ { 0 } = D _ { 0 } \mathrm { ~ i f ~ } n _ { w } = n _ { u }$ and $\hat { A } _ { 0 } = A _ { 0 } - K _ { 0 } C _ { 0 } , \hat { B } _ { 0 } =$ $\begin{array} { r l } { [ K _ { 0 } } & { { } B _ { 0 } - K _ { 0 } D _ { 0 } ] , \hat { C } _ { 0 } = C _ { 0 } , \hat { D } _ { 0 } = [ 0 \quad D _ { 0 } ] } \end{array}$ if $n _ { w } = n _ { u } + n _ { y }$ . When $\mathbf { w } ( s ) = [ \mathbf { y } ^ { T } ( s ) , \mathbf { u } ^ { T } ( s ) ] ^ { T }$ the predictor (3) corresponds to stationary Kalman-filter and it is an optimal predictor of ${ \bf y } ( t )$ in the mean square error sense. Formally, instead of assuming the existence of (2), for the data generator we use the following assumption, which is essentially equivalent to the existence of (2).

Assumption 2.2. Let ${ \bf y } ( t )$ and ${ \bf \delta u } ( t )$ be generated by an autonomous stochastic LTI system

$$
\mathbf { x } ( t + 1 ) = A _ { g } \mathbf { x } ( t ) + K _ { g } \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) , \quad \left[ \mathbf { y } ( t ) \right] = C _ { g } \mathbf { x } ( t ) + \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t )\tag{4}
$$

with $A _ { q } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n \times n } , K _ { q } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n \times n _ { \mathbf { e } } } , C _ { q } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { \mathbf { e } } \times n }$ , for some $n > 0 ,$ , and $n _ { \mathbf { e } } = n _ { \mathbf { y } } + n _ { \mathbf { u } } \ge 2 , A _ { g }$ and $A _ { g } - \check { K } _ { g } C _ { g }$ are Schur. Moreover, ${ \bf x } ( t )$ has finite variance, ${ \bf e } _ { g } ( t )$ is independent $o f \mathbf { x } ( t ) f o r$ all ${ \dot { t } } \in \mathbb { Z } ,$ and $\mathbf { e } _ { g }$ is square integrable, i.i.d process, $\| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 }$ is sub-Gaussian and the sub-Gaussian norm of $\| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } I ^ { 4 3 } ,$ , Definition 2.6.4] is denoted by $\| \mathbf { e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } }$

If ${ \bf e } _ { g } ( t )$ is sub-Gaussian, then $\| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 }$ is also sub-Gaussian, see [21, Lemma 1]. Under Assumption 2.2, y and u are jointly stationary and square integrable processes [4]. Under mild conditions, Assumption 2.2 is equivalent to the existence of an LTI system (2), see Remark $\mathrm { A } . 2$ in Appendix A. Assumption 2.2 is more convenient, and it is often used in system identification [22, Chapter 9].

Measure of prediction accuracy: Following standard practice in system identification [26], we measure the predictive accuracy using a lossfunction $\ell : \dot { \mathbb { R } } ^ { n _ { y } } \times \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { y } } \dot {  }  0 , \infty $ which is continuous and satisfies $\bar { \ell } ( y , y ) = 0$ . Typical choices are $\ell ( y , \hat { y } ) = \| y - \hat { y } \| _ { 2 } .$ , to which we refer to as $\ell _ { 2 }$ loss, and $\ell ( y , \hat { y } ) = \| y - \hat { y } \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 }$ to which we refer to as the quadratic loss.

Assumption 2.3 (Qudaratic or Lipschitz loss functions). The lossfunction ℓ is either quadratic or it is L -Lipschitz, i.e.,for all $y _ { 1 } , y _ { 2 } , \hat { y _ { 1 } } , \hat { y _ { 2 } } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { y } } , | \ell ( y _ { 1 } , \hat { y _ { 1 } } ) - \ell ( y _ { 2 } , \hat { y _ { 2 } } ) | \leq L _ { \ell } ( \| y _ { 1 } - y _ { 2 } \| \hat { 2 } + \| \hat { y } _ { 1 } - \hat { y } _ { 2 } \| _ { 2 } )$

We define the true error as the limit

$$
C ( \boldsymbol { \theta } ) \triangleq \operatorname* { l i m } _ { t _ { 0 }  - \infty } \mathbf { E } [ \ell ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) ) ] .\tag{5}
$$

The true error captures the long-term, steady-state prediction error obtained during the deployment of the predictor on an increasing number of past inputs.

Lemma 2.4. Let Assumptions 2.2, 2.1, and 2.3 hold. Then the limits $\begin{array} { r } { \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t ) = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { t _ { 0 }  - \infty } \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) } \end{array}$ and $\begin{array} { r } { \operatorname* { l i m } _ { t  \infty } ( \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ( t ) - \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ( t \mid 0 ) ) = 0 } \end{array}$ exist in the mean-square sensefor all t and θ, the process $\hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t )$ is stationary, and $\begin{array} { r } { \mathcal { L } ( \mathbf { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ) = \dot { \mathbf { E } } [ \ell ( \mathbf { \boldsymbol { y } } ( t ) , \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ( t ) ) ] = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { t  \infty } \mathbf { \dot { E } } [ \ell ( \mathbf { \boldsymbol { y } } ( t ) , \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ( t \mid 0 ) ) ] } \end{array}$

The proof of Lemma 2.4 is presented in Appendix A. Intuitively, $\hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t )$ is the output predicted by the LTI system corresponding to $\theta ,$ , if the latter is started from zero at $- \infty$ , and as t grows, $\hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t )$ and $\hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t \mid 0 )$ converge to each other.

The Learning Problem is to find a parameter $\theta _ { \star }$ from sampled data D of the sequence of random variables $\{ \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \mathbf { u } ( t ) \} _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 }$ which minimizes the true error, i.e., $\theta _ { \star } \in \arg$ min $\mathcal { L } ( \boldsymbol { \theta } )$ . Note that we do not assume the knowledge of the matrices $A _ { g } , K _ { g } , C _ { g }$ and noise process $\mathbf { e } _ { g }$ when learning.

Since the true error is unknown, a standard practice is to use the empirical error

$$
\hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) = \frac { 1 } { N } \sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \ell ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) ( \boldsymbol \omega ) , \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t | 0 ) ( \boldsymbol \omega ) )\tag{6}
$$

as a proxy for the true error, and try to find a model $\hat { \theta }$ for which the empirical error $\hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \hat { \boldsymbol { \theta } } )$ is small. While doing so, we would like to keep the generalization gap $\mathcal { L } ( \theta ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta )$ , i.e., the difference between a model’s performance on the training data (empirical error) and its expected performance (true error) on unseen test data, small. The PAC-Bayesian approach provides a framework to derive such guarantees.

## 2.3 Constants used in PAC-bounds

In order to present the main result, we will need several constants, many of which have an interesting system-theoretic interpretation.

Definition 2.5 (Convolution and $\ell _ { 1 }$ norm). Let $\alpha = \{ \alpha ( k ) \} _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty }$ be a sequence ofmatrices. Define the $\ell _ { 1 }$ norm $\| \alpha \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }$ and the weakly dependent mixing coefficient $\left( \mathrm { W D M } \bar { \mathrm { C } } \right) \theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha ) \check { \mathbf { \Gamma } }$ of α as

$$
\| \alpha \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \| \alpha ( k ) \| _ { 2 } , \quad \theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha ) = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } k \| \alpha ( k ) \| _ { 2 } .
$$

$I f \| \alpha \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } < \infty$ and r is a stationary square integrable stochastic process, then define the convolution $( \alpha \star \mathbf { r } )$ of α with r as the stochastic process $\begin{array} { r } { ( \bar { \alpha ^ { } } { \star \mathbf { r } } ) ( t ) = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \bar { \alpha } ( k ) \mathbf { r } ( t - k ) } \end{array}$ . By [4, Theorem 1.1], $( \alpha \star \mathbf { r } )$ is well-defined and stationary.

Intuitively, the $\ell _ { 1 }$ norm of a sequence is the extension of the standard norm on the space of absolutely summable scalar sequences to the case of matrix valued ones. The convolution $( \alpha \star \mathbf { r } )$ represents the response of a linear filter with impulse response α to the noise r, and $\| \alpha \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }$ represents the $\ell _ { 1 }$ norm of the filter. If r is bounded, $\mathrm { i . e . , } \| \mathbf { r } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { \cdot } < C$ , then the convolution $( \alpha \star \mathbf { r } ) ( t )$ is bounded, $\mathrm { i . e . , } \| ( \alpha \star \mathbf { r } ) \| _ { 2 } < \| \alpha \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } C$ , and it is a weakly dependent process, in the sense of [3], whose mixing coefficients $\{ \theta _ { \infty , n } ( 1 ) \} _ { n = 1 } ^ { \infty }$ are bounded by $2 \theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha ) C , [ 3$ , Proposition 4.12]. Intuitively, the smaller $\theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha )$ is, the closer $( \alpha \star \mathbf { r } ) ( t )$ is to being i.i.d., if r is i.i.d. If α is the sequence of Markov parameters of a stable LTI system, then both $\| \alpha \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }$ and $\theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha )$ are finite and can be upper bounded in terms of bounds on the norms of the system matrices and the spectral radius of the system matrix $A ,$ , see Remark B.1.

Definition 2.6 (Auxiliary definitions). Let us consider the Markov-parameters $\alpha _ { g }$ and α ofthe data generator (4) and predictor (1) corresponding to $\theta \in \Theta$ respectively:

$$
\alpha _ { g } ( k ) = \left\{ \begin{array} { r l r } { { C _ { g } A _ { g } ^ { k - 1 } K _ { g } } } & { { k > 0 } } \\ { { I } } & { { k = 0 } } \end{array} \right. , \quad \alpha _ { \theta } ( k ) = \left\{ \begin{array} { r l r } { { \widehat C _ { \theta } \widehat A _ { \theta } ^ { k - 1 } \widehat B _ { \theta } } } & { { k > 0 } } \\ { { \widehat D _ { \theta } } } & { { k = 0 } } \end{array} \right.\tag{7}
$$

Let $\bar { \alpha } ( k ) = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \theta \in \Theta } \| \alpha _ { \theta } ( k ) \| _ { 2 }$ . With notation as above

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { G _ { e } ( \theta ) \triangleq ( 1 + \| \alpha _ { \theta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ) \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \quad G _ { e , 1 } ( \theta ) \triangleq \theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha _ { \theta } ) \| \alpha \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } + \theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha _ { g } ) ( \| \alpha _ { \theta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } + 1 ) } \\ & { G _ { e } \triangleq ( 1 + \| \bar { \alpha } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ) \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \quad G _ { e , 1 } \triangleq \theta _ { \infty } ( \bar { \alpha } ) \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } + \theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha _ { g } ) ( \| \bar { \alpha } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } + 1 ) } \end{array}
$$

For the definition of the upcoming bounds, we are going to use the quantities $\| \alpha \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha )$ for $\alpha \in \{ \alpha _ { q } , \bar { \alpha } \} \cup \{ \alpha _ { \theta } \mid \theta \in \Theta \}$ and the constants $\bar { G _ { e } } ( \theta ) , \bar { G _ { e , 1 } } ( \theta ) , \bar { G _ { e } } , \bar { G _ { e , 1 } }$ defined above. Note that all these quantities are well-defined, see Lemma B.2. In addition, they are related to the true outputs $\mathbf { y } ( t )$ and predicted outputs $\hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t ) , \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } )$ as follows: $\left[ \mathbf { y } ^ { T } ( t ) \quad \mathbf { u } ^ { T } ( t ) \right] ^ { T } = ( \alpha _ { g } \star \mathbf { e } _ { g } ) ( t )$ $\hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t ) = ( \alpha _ { \theta } { \star } \mathbf { w } ) ( t ) , \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) = ( \alpha _ { \theta , t - t _ { 0 } } { \star } \mathbf { w } ) ( t )$ . The formal relationship between the constants is provided in Lemma B.3, Appendix B. The interpretation of the various terms appearing in Definition 2.6 is as follows

$\| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha _ { g } )$ are the $\ell _ { 1 }$ norm and the mixing coefficients of Markov-parameters of the data generator: both quantities decrease with stability of the data generator, see Remark B.1.

$\| \alpha _ { \theta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }$ is the $\ell _ { 1 }$ norm of the predictor (1). This term decreases with the spectral radius of the predictor, see Remark B.1.

$\theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha _ { \theta } )$ describes the mixing properties of the output generated by the predictor, and it also bounds the sum of differences between the infinite and finite past prediction $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \| \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ( t ) - \| } \end{array}$ $\hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t \mid 0 ) \mid$ . Intuitively, the smaller $\theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha _ { \theta } )$ is, the closer $\hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } \bar { ( } t \mid 0 \bar { ) }$ is to $\hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t )$ . This quantity decreases with the spectral radius of the predictor by Remark B.1.

$G _ { e , 1 } ( \theta )$ characterizes mixing properties (short memory condition [1]) of the prediction error $\mathbf { y } ( t ) - \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t )$ . Intuitively, the smaller $G _ { e , 1 } ( \theta )$ is, the more the prediction error behaves like an i.i.d process. This constant is related to stability of the generator and the predictor, the smaller the spectral radius of those systems are , the smaller this constant is.

$\lVert \bar { \alpha } \rVert _ { \ell } , \theta _ { \infty } ( \bar { \alpha } ) , G _ { e } , G _ { e , 1 }$ are uniform upper bounds on $\| \alpha _ { \theta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha _ { \theta } ) , G _ { e } ( \theta ) , G _ { e , 1 } ( \theta )$ respectively.

## 3 Main result

We start by presenting the main result of the paper, which is the following theorem.

Theorem 3.1 (Main result). Let Assumptions 2.2, 2.3 and 2.1 hold. For any a prior distribution π over Θ, confidence level $\delta \in ( 0 , 0 . 5 )$ , integer $\epsilon \in \{ - 1 , 1 \}$ , and $N \geq K _ { \delta }$ , the inequality

$$
\begin{array} { l } { \displaystyle \forall \rho \in \mathcal { M } _ { \pi } : \epsilon E _ { \theta \sim \rho } \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) \leq \epsilon E _ { \theta \sim \rho } \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) + } \\ { \displaystyle \quad \frac { 1 } { \lambda } \Big ( \mathrm { K L } ( \rho \| \pi ) + \ln \frac { C _ { \delta } } { \delta } + \frac { 1 } { 2 } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \ln E _ { \theta \sim \pi } \exp \left( \mathcal { C } _ { i } ( 2 \lambda , \theta , N , \delta , \epsilon ) \right) \Big ) } \end{array}\tag{8}
$$

holds with probability at least $1 - 2 \delta$ over data, with $\mathrm { K L } ( \rho \| \pi ) = E _ { \theta \sim \rho }$ ln $\frac { \rho ( \theta ) } { \pi ( \theta ) }$ the Kullback–Leibler divergence and the constants $K _ { \delta } , \mathcal { C } _ { i } ( \lambda , \theta , N , \delta , \epsilon ) , i = 1 , 2$ and $C _ { \delta }$ are defined as follows:

Bounded noise: $I f \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { \infty } \leq c _ { \mathbf { e } } ,$ , then $C _ { \delta } = 1 , K _ { \delta } = 2 , \lambda > 0$ , and

$$
\mathcal { C } _ { i } ( \lambda , \theta , N , \delta , \epsilon ) \triangleq \mathscr { C } _ { b , i } ( \lambda , \theta , L ( \theta ) , c _ { \textup e } , N ) \triangleq \frac { \mathscr { C } _ { m , b , i } ( \lambda , \alpha _ { \theta } , G _ { e } ( \theta ) + 2 G _ { e , 1 } ( \theta ) , L ( \theta ) , c _ { \textup e } ) } { N }
$$

$$
L ( \theta ) \triangleq { \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { 2 G _ { e } ( \theta ) c _ { \mathbf { e } } { \sqrt { n _ { \mathbf { e } } } } } & { \ \ell ~ i s ~ q u a d r a t i c ~ l o s s } \\ { L _ { \ell } } & { \ \ell ~ i s ~ L _ { \ell } { - } L i p s c h i t z } \end{array} \right. }
$$

$$
\mathcal { C } _ { m , b , i } ( \lambda , \alpha , X , L , c _ { \mathbf { e } } ) \triangleq \left. \begin{array} { r l } { \lambda L c _ { \mathbf { e } } \sqrt { n _ { \mathbf { e } } } \theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha ) \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } } & { i = 1 } \\ { \lambda ^ { 2 } \frac { L ^ { 2 } } { 2 } X ( \sqrt { n _ { \mathbf { e } } } c _ { \mathbf { e } } ) ^ { 2 } } & { i = 2 } \end{array} \right.
$$

Lipschitz loss: If ℓ is L<sub>ℓ</sub>-Lipschitz, then $\begin{array} { r } { C _ { \delta } = 1 , K _ { \delta } = 2 , 0 < \lambda < \frac { N } { 1 6 L \ell } } \end{array}$ , and

$$
\mathcal { C } _ { i } ( \lambda , \theta , N , \delta , \epsilon ) \triangleq \mathcal { C } _ { u b , i } ( \lambda , \theta , L ( \theta ) , N ) \triangleq \frac { 1 } { 2 } \mathcal { C } _ { b , i } \left( 2 \lambda , \theta , L ( \theta ) , \frac { 2 \ln ( N ) } { \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } K } , N \right) + \bar { \mathcal { C } } _ { u b } ( \lambda , L ( \theta ) , N )
$$

$$
\mathcal { \bar { C } } _ { u b } ( \lambda , L ( \theta ) , N ) \triangleq \frac { \lambda ^ { 2 } 3 2 L ( \theta ) ^ { 2 } C _ { u , 1 } } { N } + \frac { 4 \lambda L ( \theta ) C _ { u , 2 } \ln ( N ) } { N } = \frac { \lambda ^ { 2 } 3 2 L _ { \ell } ^ { 2 } C _ { u , 1 } } { N } + \frac { 4 \lambda L _ { \ell } C _ { u , 2 } \ln ( N ) } { N }
$$

where the constants, $K , C _ { u , j } , j = 1 ,$ , 2 are defined as follows:

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { C _ { u , 1 } \triangleq \bar { \Psi } _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( \| \bar { \alpha } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ) + \bar { \Psi } _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ) , \quad \bar { \Psi } _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( s ) = e ^ { 1 4 ^ { 2 } s ^ { 2 } \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } ^ { 2 } + 3 s \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } } } \\ & { C _ { u , 2 } \triangleq \bar { \Psi } _ { \mathbf { e } } ( \| \bar { \alpha } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ) \frac { \| \bar { \alpha } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ^ { 2 } \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } } { K } + \bar { \Psi } _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ) \frac { \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } } { K } , \qquad K = \operatorname* { m i n } \{ 1 , \| \bar { \alpha } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \} } \end{array}\tag{9}
$$

Quadratic loss: $i f \ell$ is the quadratic loss, then $\begin{array} { r } { C _ { \delta } = \frac { 2 } { 3 } , K _ { \delta } = 3 , 0 < \lambda < \frac { N } { 1 6 L _ { q d } } , } \end{array}$

$$
\mathcal { C } _ { i } ( \lambda , \theta , N , \delta , \epsilon ) \triangleq \frac { \lambda L _ { q d } ^ { 2 } \operatorname* { m a x } \{ 0 , \epsilon \} } { 2 N } + \mathcal { C } _ { u b , i } \left( \lambda , \theta , L _ { q d } , N \right) , \quad L _ { q d } = 1 2 G _ { e } \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } \sqrt { \ln \left( \frac { 3 N ^ { 2 } } { 2 \delta } \right) }
$$

For $\epsilon = 1 , ( 8 )$ bounds the average generalization gap $E _ { \theta \sim \rho } ( \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) )$ ; for $\epsilon = - 1$ , it bounds $E _ { \theta \sim \rho } ( \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) - \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) )$ ). The latter is useful for deriving oracle inequalities. By applying the union bound to both cases, we obtain a bound on $\left| E _ { \theta \sim \rho } ( \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) ) \right|$ . Note that except for the quadratic loss case with potentially unbounded data, $\mathcal { C } _ { i } ( \lambda , \theta , N , \delta , \epsilon )$ is independent of δ and ϵ.

Intuition behind the proof: The proof of Theorem 3.1 is in Appendix C. The key idea is to decompose the generalization gap using an auxiliary empirical error $\begin{array} { r } { V _ { N } ( \theta ) = \frac { 1 } { N } \sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \ell ( { \bf y } ( t ) , \hat { \bf y } _ { \theta } ( t ) ) } \end{array}$ where the predictor has run for infinite time (started at $t _ { 0 } = - \infty )$ . Since $\mathbf { E } [ \dot { V _ { N } } ( \overline { { \theta } } ) ] = \mathcal { L } ( \theta )$ , we have $\epsilon ( \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) ) = \epsilon ( \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) - V _ { N } ( \theta ) ) + \epsilon ( V _ { N } ( \theta ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) )$ . The constants $\exp ( \mathcal { C } _ { 1 } )$ and $\exp ( \mathcal { C } _ { 2 } )$ bound the moment generating functions of these two differences. The PAC-Bayesian bound then follows by standard concentration arguments [2, Chapter 2].

Bounded case: $\mathcal { C } _ { 1 }$ is obtained by direct calculation, and $\mathcal { C } _ { 2 }$ via Rios’s concentration inequality for weakly-dependent processes [1, Prop. 4.2], improving upon constants in [11, 8].

Unbounded case: We employ a truncation approach [3]. Define truncated data $\bar { \mathbf { y } } ( t ) , \bar { \mathbf { u } } ( t )$ generated from truncated noise $\bar { \mathbf { e } } _ { g } ( t ) = \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \chi ( \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| \le C )$ with $\begin{array} { r } { C = \frac { 2 \ln ( N ) } { K \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } } } \end{array}$ , where $K = \operatorname* { m i n } \{ 1 , \| \bar { \alpha } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \}$ We bound the moment generating functions of the differences $\begin{array} { r } { \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \theta \in \Theta } | \hat { \bar { \mathcal { L } } } _ { N } ( \theta ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) | } \end{array}$ and su ${ \mathfrak { p } } _ { \theta \in \Theta } | V _ { N } ( \theta ) - { \bar { V } } _ { N } ( \theta )$ | by $\exp ( \bar { \mathcal { C } } _ { u b } ( \lambda , L \ell , N ) )$ . Combining with the bounded-case bounds yields PAC-Bayesian guarantees for unbounded data.

Quadratic case: We show that with probability at least $\textstyle 1 - { \frac { 2 \delta } { 3 } }$ over data, the prediction errors satisfy $\| \mathbf { y } ( t ) - \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } \leq C$ and $\| \mathbf { y } ( t ) - \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid 0 ) \| _ { 2 } \leq C$ for $\begin{array} { r } { C = 6 G _ { e } \| \mathbf e _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } \sqrt { \ln ( \frac { 3 N ^ { 2 } } { 2 \delta } ) } } \end{array}$ . The empirical quadratic loss then coincides with the empirical error for the truncated Lipschitz loss $\ell _ { C } { \dot { ( } y , y ^ { \prime } ) } \overset { * } { = } \operatorname* { m i n } \{ \| y - y ^ { \prime } \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } , C ^ { 2 } \}$ . The result follows by union bound and the Lipschitz case, noting that the true error difference between the quadratic loss and the truncated loss is ${ \cal O } ( C ^ { 2 } / N )$

Interpretation and $O \big ( \frac { \ln ( N ) ^ { r } } { N } \big )$ rates: The interpretation of Theorem 3.1 is similar to other PAC-Bayesian bounds [2]: we view learning algorithms as choices of parameters from a data-dependent probability density on model parameters, referred to as posterior $\rho ^ { \stackrel { \cdot } { D } }$ , for which the right-hand side of (8) is small for $\epsilon = 1$ . One option is to choose $\rho ^ { \mathcal { D } }$ as the Gibbs posterior which minimizes of the right-hand side of (8) and which is given by [2, Definition 2.1]:

$$
\rho _ { \mathrm { G i b b s } } \triangleq \operatorname { a r g m i n } _ { \rho \in { \cal M } _ { \pi } } \left( E _ { \theta \sim \rho } \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) + \frac { 1 } { \lambda } \mathrm { K L } ( \rho \| \pi ) \right) , \quad \rho _ { \mathrm { G i b b s } } ( \theta ) = \frac { \pi ( \theta ) \exp ( - \lambda \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) ) } { E _ { \theta \sim \pi } \exp ( - \lambda \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) ) } ,
$$

Once a posterior $\rho ^ { \mathcal { D } }$ is obtained, the learned model $\theta _ { \star }$ can be chosen as follows:

Mean posterior (MEP): as the mean of the posterior $\rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } , \mathrm { i . e . } \ \theta _ { \star } = E _ { \theta \sim \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } } \theta .$

Single draw: draw $\theta _ { \star }$ randomly from $\rho ^ { \mathcal { D } }$ , and

Maximum posterior (MAP): choose $\theta _ { \star }$ as $\theta _ { \star } = \mathrm { a r g m a x } \rho ^ { { D } } ( \theta )$ . For $\rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } = \rho ^ { \mathrm { G i b b s } }$ , it minimizes the empirical error regularized using the prior, i.e., $\begin{array} { r } { \theta _ { \star } = \arg \operatorname* { m i n } _ { \theta \in \Theta } \left( \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) - \frac { 1 } { \lambda } \ln \pi ( \theta ) \right) } \end{array}$

These choices represent well-known classes of system identification algorithms, such as regularized PEM, maximimum likelihood estimation for state-space models, and Bayesian methods, see Remarks D.8, D.5, and D.6 in Appendix D.5.

Theorem 3.1 bounds the average generalization gap $E _ { \theta \sim \rho } \left( { \mathcal { L } } ( \theta ) - { \hat { \mathcal { L } } } _ { N } ( \theta ) \right)$ by a sum of two terms: the KL divergence ${ \scriptstyle { \frac { 1 } { \lambda } } } \mathrm { K L } ( \rho \| \pi )$ between the posterior and the prior, and a complexity term $\mathcal { C } _ { i } ( 2 \lambda , \theta , N , \delta , \epsilon )$ that depends on the data and the prior. For bounded noise, $\begin{array} { r } { \mathcal { C } _ { i } = \dot { O } ( 1 / \dot { N } ) ; } \end{array}$ for Lipschitz loss, $\begin{array} { r } { \mathcal { C } _ { i } = O ( \frac { \ln N ) } { N } ) } \end{array}$ ; and for quadratic loss, $\begin{array} { r } { \mathcal { C } _ { i } = O ( \frac { ( \ln N ) ^ { 3 } } { N } ) } \end{array}$ . By choosing $\begin{array} { r } { \lambda = \frac { \sqrt { N } } { L ( \ln N ) ^ { r } } } \end{array}$ with appropriately selected L and $r ,$ and thus forcing ${ \scriptstyle { \frac { 1 } { \lambda } } } \mathrm { K L } ( \rho \| \pi )$ to tend to zero as $N  \infty$ , we obtain an $O \left( \frac { ( \ln N ) ^ { r } } { \sqrt { N } } \right)$ :

Corollary 3.2 $( O ( ( \ln ( N ) ) ^ { r } / \sqrt { N } )$ bound). With the notation and assumptions of Theorem 3.1,

$$
\forall \rho \in { \mathcal { M } } _ { \pi } : \epsilon E _ { \theta \sim \rho } { \mathcal { L } } ( \theta ) \leq \epsilon E _ { \theta \sim \rho } { \hat { \mathcal { L } } } _ { N } ( \theta ) + { \frac { L ( \ln ( N ) ) ^ { r } } { \sqrt { N } } } \left( \mathrm { K L } ( \rho | | \pi ) + \ln { \frac { C \delta } { \delta } } + { \mathcal { C } } ( \delta , \epsilon ) \right)\tag{10}
$$

holds with probability at least $1 - 2 \delta$ over data,for any prior π, $\delta \in ( 0 , 0 . 5 )$ , and for any $\epsilon \in \{ - 1 , 1 \}$ and $N \geq K _ { \delta } ,$ where $\mathcal { C } ( \delta , \epsilon ) , L , r$ are defined asfollows.

Bounded noise: $I f \| e _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { \infty } \leq c _ { \mathbf { e } } ,$ , then $r \triangleq 0 , L \triangleq 1$ , and

$$
\mathcal { C } ( \delta , \epsilon ) \triangleq \frac { 1 } { 2 } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \mathcal { C } _ { m , b , i } ( 2 , \bar { \alpha } , G _ { e } + 2 G _ { e , 1 } , L _ { b } , c _ { \mathrm { e } } ) , \quad L _ { b } \triangleq \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { 2 G _ { e } c _ { \mathrm { e } } \sqrt { n _ { \mathrm { e } } } \quad } & { \ell i s t h e q u a d r a t i c l o s s } \\ { L _ { \ell } \quad \ell i s L _ { \ell } . L i p s c h i t z } \end{array} \right.
$$

Lipschitz loss: If ℓ is L -Lipschitz, then $r \triangleq 1 , L \triangleq 1 6 L _ { \ell } ,$ and

$$
\mathcal { C } ( \delta , \epsilon ) \triangleq \bar { \mathcal { C } } _ { u b } = \left( C _ { u , 1 } + C _ { u , 2 } + \frac { 1 } { 2 } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \mathcal { C } _ { m , b , i } \left( \frac { 1 } { 4 } , \bar { \alpha } , G _ { e } + 2 G _ { e , 1 } , 1 , \frac { 1 } { \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } K } \right) \right) ,
$$

Quadaratic loss: $H \ell$ is quadratic, then $\begin{array} { r } { L \triangleq 1 9 2 G _ { e } \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } \sqrt { 2 } \sqrt { 1 + \ln \big ( \frac { 3 } { 2 \delta } \big ) } , r \triangleq 3 / 2 , } \end{array}$

$$
\mathcal { C } ( \delta , \epsilon ) \triangleq \frac { L \operatorname* { m a x } \{ 0 , \epsilon \} } { 1 2 8 } + C _ { u , 1 } + C _ { u , 2 } + \frac { 1 } { 2 } { \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } } \mathcal { C } _ { m , b , i } \left( \frac { 1 } { 4 } , \bar { \alpha } , G _ { e } + 2 G _ { e , 1 } , 1 , \frac { 1 } { \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } K } \right)
$$

The proof of Corollary 3.2 follows by substituting $\begin{array} { r } { \lambda = \frac { \sqrt { N } } { L ( \ln N ) ^ { r } } } \end{array}$ into Theorem 3.1 and simplifying the resulting bounds (see Appendix C.4 for the proof). Note that while Corollary 3.2 makes the $O \left( \frac { ( \ln N ) ^ { r } } { \sqrt { N } } \right)$ rates explicit, Theorem 3.1 may give a tighter bound even for $\begin{array} { r } { \lambda = \frac { \sqrt { N } } { L ( \ln N ) ^ { r } } \left[ 2 \right] } \end{array}$

The previous results bound the average generalization gap with respect to a posterior $\rho ^ { \mathcal { D } }$ . For practical learning, however, we are interested in the generalization gap for a specific choice of θ<sub>⋆</sub> (e.g., MEP, MAP, or a single draw from $\rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } )$ . Below, we provide such bounds when $\rho ^ { \mathcal { D } }$ is the Gibbs posterior with $\begin{array} { r } { \lambda = \frac { \hat { \sqrt { N } } } { L ( \ln N ) ^ { r } } } \end{array}$ , where $L , r$ are as in Corollary 3.2. This yields a generalization gap for θ<sub>⋆</sub> of order $O \left( \frac { ( \ln N ) ^ { r + 1 } } { \sqrt { N } } \right)$ . We now introduce the necessary assumptions for these results.

Assumption 3.3. Thefollowing conditions are used in the sequel.

(Opt) An optimal model $\begin{array} { r } { \theta _ { t r u e } = \arg \operatorname* { m i n } _ { \theta \in \Theta } \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) } \end{array}$ exists.

(LipModel) The map $\theta \mapsto \alpha _ { \theta }$ is $L _ { \alpha }$ -Lipschitz w.r.t. to the $\| \cdot \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }$ norm, $i . e .$

$$
\| \alpha _ { \theta _ { 1 } } - \alpha _ { \theta _ { 2 } } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \leq L _ { \alpha } \| \theta _ { 1 } - \theta _ { 2 } \| _ { 2 } , \quad \forall \theta _ { 1 } , \theta _ { 2 } \in \Theta .
$$

(Gauss) $\Theta = \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { \theta } }$ and π is Gaussian $\mathcal { N } ( \theta _ { m } , P )$

(Uni) Θ is compact and π is the uniform density on $\Theta .$

(SConv) The true error $\mathcal { L } ( \boldsymbol { \theta } )$ is smooth and strongly convex in θ and its Hessian is boundedfrom below m<sub>Θ</sub> $I \preceq \nabla _ { \theta } ^ { 2 } { \mathcal { L } } ( \theta )$ for all $\theta \in \Theta$

(Real) The predictor $\Sigma ( \theta _ { t r u e } )$ equals the predictor (3) corresponding to the data generator.

(PE) The spectral density $\Phi _ { \mathbf { w } }$ of w satisfies $\Phi _ { \bf w } ( i z ) \ge m _ { \bf w } I f o r a l l z \in [ - \pi , \pi ]$

Corollary 3.4. Assume the following

(1) Assumptions 2.2, 2.1, 2.3 and (Opt) and (LipModel) ofAssumption 3.3 hold, and

(2) either (Gauss) or (Uni) of Assumption 3.3 holds, and

(3) θ<sub>⋆</sub> be selected from ρ<sub>Gibbs</sub> using MEP, MAP or a single draw, and ρ<sub>Gibbs</sub> is computed using $\begin{array} { r } { \lambda = \lambda _ { N } = \frac { \sqrt { N } } { 2 L ( \ln ( N ) ) ^ { r } } } \end{array}$ with $r , L , K _ { \delta }$ are as defined in Corollary 3.2.

(4) Furthermore, if θ<sub>⋆</sub> is chosen using MEP, then assume that (SConv) of Assumption 3.3 holds.

Consider the constants $\mathcal { C } _ { e m } ( \delta , \sigma ) , \mathcal { C } _ { o r } ( \delta , \sigma ) , r _ { L i p , 1 } , r _ { L i p , 2 }$ as in Table 1, and consider the inequalities

$$
E m p i r i c a l b o u n d : ~ \mathcal { L } ( \theta _ { \star } ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta _ { \star } ) \leq \frac { L ( \ln ( N ) ) ^ { r + 1 } } { \sqrt { N } } C _ { e m } ( \theta _ { \star } , \delta )\tag{11}
$$

$$
O r a c l e b o u n d : \mathcal { L } ( \theta _ { \star } ) - \mathcal { L } ( \theta _ { t r u e } ) \leq \frac { L ( \ln ( N ) ) ^ { r + 1 } } { \sqrt { N } } \mathcal { C } _ { o r } ( \delta , \sigma )\tag{12}
$$

$$

In summary, for any outcome $\theta _ { \star }$ obtained via MAP, MEP, or a single draw from the Gibbs posterior, Corollary 3.4 yields $O \left( \frac { ( \ln N ) ^ { r + 1 } } { \sqrt { N } } \right)$ bounds on: (1) the generalization gap (11), (2) the excess true error over the optimal model (12), and (3) the parameter estimation error (13)–(14). The constants for the learning bounds, as given by Corollary 3.4, are in Table 1. The influence of system properties and convergence rates is discussed in Section 4.

Intuition behind the proof: The proof of Corollary 3.4 is in Appendix D. For MAP, (11)–(12) are derived from Corollary 3.2 by constructing suitable data-dependent posteriors and applying oracle inequality techniques from [2, Theorem 4.1]. For single draw and MEP, similar arguments apply using [2, Theorems 2.7 and Corollary 2.6] combined with the moment generating function bounds from the proof of Theorem 3.1. Finally, the parameter estimation bounds (13)–(14) follow from the oracle inequality (12) by relating the parameter error to the loss difference through strong convexity (for (13)) or persistence of excitation (for (14)).

## 4 Discussion on the bound and conclusions

In this paper, we derived PAC-Bayesian error bounds for stochastic LTI systems with inputs and sub-Gaussian noise.

The convergence rates are fastest for bounded noise, slower for Lipschitz losses, and slowest for quadratic losses with general sub-Gaussian noise (see Table 2). All bounds in Theorem 3.1, Corollary 3.2, and Corollary 3.4 improve with increased system stability, lower sub-Gaussian noise norm, and depend logarithmically on the confidence level δ via ln $( 1 / \delta )$

Specifically, the bounds increase monotonically with the sub-Gaussian norm $\| \mathbf { e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } }$ of the noise, and with the $\ell _ { 1 }$ norms and mixing coefficients of the Markov parameters of both the predictors and the data generator $( \| \alpha _ { \theta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha _ { \theta } ) , \theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha _ { g } ) )$ . Smaller $\ell _ { 1 }$ norms and mixing coefficients correspond to more stable systems and weaker dependence on past inputs, making learning easier. Similarly, a smaller sub-Gaussian norm (i.e., lower noise variance) yields tighter bounds.

In summary, the bounds are tighter for less noisy and more stable systems, aligning with intuition: learning is easier when the system is less noisy and more stable.

Additionally, if the goal is to bound not only the generalization gap but also the parameter estimation error (in terms of the $H _ { 2 }$ distance between the true and learned systems (14), or the Euclidean distance between parameters (13)), the bounds also depend on the persistence of excitation of the input w $( m _ { \mathbf { w } } )$ and the identifiability constant $( m _ { \Theta } )$ . Larger values of $m _ { \mathbf { w } }$ (richer input) and $m _ { \Theta }$ (greater separation between models) yield tighter bounds. The bound (14) can also be used to bound the system matrices (up to similarity), see Remark D.10, Appendix D.5.

For parameter estimation error, our obtained rate $O ( ( \ln N ) ^ { r + 1 } / \sqrt { N } )$ is more conservative than classical asymptotic theory [26, 4] (which yields $O ( 1 / \sqrt { N } )$ asymptotic consistency) and existing finite-sample bounds [32, 24, 39, 42](which achieve $O ( \ln N / { \sqrt { N } } ) )$ . However, this is the expected price for generality: our bound applies to a broad class of learning algorithms, provides nonasymptotic guarantees with high probability, and simultaneously bounds the generalization gap (properties not offered by prior results). In addition, most existing finite-sample bounds are algorithmspecific (e.g., for least-squares only) and do not address the generalization gap.

## A Proofs of Section 2

Remark A.1 (LTI in innovation form). Let us assume that $\mathbf { e } _ { g }$ has zero mean. It follows that ${ \bf e } _ { g } ( t )$ is the innovation process of $[ \mathbf { y } ^ { T } ( t ) \mathbf { u } ^ { T } ( t ) ] ^ { T }$ and (4) is in the so called (forward) innovationform, see [22, Section 8.5.2]. Intuitively, it means that $\mathbf { e } _ { g }$ is the difference between $[ \mathbf { y } ^ { T } ( t ) \mathbf { u } ^ { T } ( t ) ] ^ { T }$ and its best linear prediction based on its own past values.

Remark A.2 (Equivalence of (4) and (2)). Let us assume that $\mathbf { e } _ { g }$ and $\mathbf { e } _ { s }$ are both zero mean. More precisely, if there is no feedback from y to u according to Definition 17.1.1. of [25], then (2) holds and by [10] the matrices $A _ { 0 } , B _ { 0 } , C _ { 0 } , K _ { 0 } , C _ { 0 } , D _ { 0 }$ can be computedfrom the matrices $A _ { g } , K _ { g } , C _ { g }$

Conversely, if (2) holds and u(t) is generated by an ARMA process, and there is nofeedbackfrom y(t) to u(t), then Assumption 2.2 holds and $A _ { g } , K _ { g } , C _ { g }$ can be computedfrom those of (2) and the ARMA representation ofu(t), again by reversing the equationsfrom [10]. As a passing remark, if there is afeedbackfrom y(t) to u(t) and u(t) is generated by an LTI systems driven by y(t), i.e.,

$$
{ \bf s _ { u } } ( t + 1 ) = A _ { u } { \bf s _ { u } } ( t ) + K _ { u } { \bf y } ( t ) , \quad { \bf u } ( t ) = C _ { u } { \bf s _ { u } } ( t )\tag{15}
$$

then again the generator described in Assumption 2.2 can be obtained by adding (15) to (2).

Remark A.3 (Methods for stable parametrisations). The construction of[33, Section 3.2.3] relies on canonicalforms based on structure indices, to which the diffeomorphismfrom [18] between general and stable parametersations are applied. Intuitively, the latter transformation maps $( A , \bar { B , C , D } )$ to $\textstyle { \bigl ( } { \frac { A } { \mu } } , B , C , D { \bigr ) }$ , where $\mu$ is the maximum of moduli of the eigenvalues of A. The construction of [28, Section 4.6] uses Ober’s balanced canonical forms, and it relies on fixing the observability and controllability Grammians to being equal and diagonal. That is, stability is assured by using diagonal quadratic Lyapunov functions. In this case, by fixing upper bounds on the diagnonals of the Grammians and on the norms of the system matrices occurring in the parameterisation, we ensure existence ofconstants $M , C , \gamma$ mentioned in Assumption 2.1.

ProofofLemma 2.4. We have $\begin{array} { r l r } { { \bf y } _ { \theta } ( t | t _ { 0 } ) } & { { } = } & { \sum _ { k = t _ { 0 } } ^ { t - 1 } H _ { \theta } ( t - 1 - k ) { \bf w } ( k ) + D _ { \theta } { \bf w } ( t ) = } \end{array}$ $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { t - 1 - t _ { 0 } } H _ { \theta } ( k ) \mathbf { w } ( t - 1 - k ) + D _ { \theta } \mathbf { w } ( t ) } \end{array}$ with the impulse response $H _ { \theta } ( k ) \triangleq C _ { \theta } A _ { \theta } ^ { k } B _ { \theta }$ for $k \geq 0$ Because $A _ { \theta }$ is Schur, there exist $M \ > \ 0$ and $\rho ~ \in ~ ( 0 , 1 )$ such that $\| A _ { \theta } ^ { k } \| _ { 2 } ~ \le ~ M \rho ^ { k }$ , hence $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { k = s } ^ { \infty } \| H _ { \theta } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } \leq \| C _ { \theta } \| _ { 2 } \| B _ { \theta } \| _ { 2 } M { \frac { \rho ^ { s } } { 1 - \rho } }  ( } \end{array}$ as $s  \infty$ . For $t _ { 1 } ~ < ~ t _ { 2 } ~ \le ~ t$ we get, using stationarity of w $( \sigma _ { \mathbf { w } } ^ { 2 } = \mathbf { E } \| \mathbf { w } ( s ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } )$ and setting $\beta = \rho ^ { 2 } .$ , that

$$
\mathbf { E } [  \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t  t _ { 2 } ) - \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t  t _ { 1 } )  _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } ] = \mathbf { E } [  { \sum _ { m = t - t _ { 2 } } ^ { t - 1 - t _ { 1 } } H _ { \theta } ( m ) } \mathbf { w } ( t - 1 - m )  _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } ]\tag{16a}
$$

$$
\leq ( t _ { 2 } - t _ { 1 } ) \mathbf { E } \left[ \sum _ { m = t - t _ { 2 } } ^ { t - 1 - t _ { 1 } } \| H _ { \theta } ( m ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } \| \mathbf { w } ( t - 1 - m ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } \right]\tag{16b}
$$

$$
\leq \sigma _ { \mathbf { w } } ^ { 2 } ( \| C _ { \theta } \| _ { 2 } \| B _ { \theta } \| _ { 2 } M ) ^ { 2 } ( t _ { 2 } - t _ { 1 } ) \sum _ { m = t - t _ { 2 } } ^ { t - 1 - t _ { 1 } } \beta ^ { m }\tag{16c}
$$

$$
= \sigma _ { \mathbf { w } } ^ { 2 } ( \| C _ { \theta } \| _ { 2 } \| B _ { \theta } \| _ { 2 } M ) ^ { 2 } \frac { \beta ^ { t } } { 1 - \beta } ( t _ { 2 } - t _ { 1 } ) ( \beta ^ { - t _ { 2 } } - \beta ^ { - t _ { 1 } } )\tag{16d}
$$

Therefore $\{ \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \} _ { t _ { 0 } \leq t }$ is Cauchy and hence converges in mean square to $\begin{array} { r l } { \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) } & { { } = } \end{array}$ $\begin{array} { r l r } { \sum _ { k = - \infty } ^ { t - 1 } H _ { \theta } ( t - 1 - k ) { \bf w } ( k ) } & { { } + } & { D _ { \theta } { \bf w } ( t ) = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } H _ { \theta } ( k ) { \bf w } ( t - 1 - k ) + D _ { \theta } { \bf w } ( t ) } \end{array}$ . This representation shows that $\mathbf { y } _ { \theta }$ is the output of a causal, time-invariant, stable LTI filter (with impulse response $\{ H _ { \theta } ( k ) \}$ and feed-through $D _ { \theta } )$ driven by the stationary input $\mathbf { w } .$ . Hence $\mathbf { y } _ { \theta }$ is stationary, since stable LTI filtering preserves stationarity. Moreover, it follows that

$$
\mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) - \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) = \sum _ { k = t - t _ { 0 } } ^ { \infty } H _ { \theta } ( k ) \mathbf { w } ( t - 1 - k ) .\tag{17}
$$

so ${ \bf y } _ { \theta } ( t ) - { \bf y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid 0 )  0$ in mean square as $t \to \infty$ by arguments as in (16).

Finally, consider

$$
\left. \mathbf { E } \big [ \ell \big ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \big ) \big ] - \mathbf { E } \big [ \ell \big ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \big ) \big ] \right. \leq \mathbf { E } \big [ \big \vert \ell \big ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \big ) - \ell \big ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \big ) \big \vert \big ]\tag{18}
$$

If ℓ is L<sub>ℓ</sub>-Lipschitz, then the right hand side of (18) is bounded by $L _ { \ell } \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) - \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \| _ { 2 } ]$ , and hence, as ${ \bf y } _ { \theta } ( t ) - { \bf y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid 0 )  0$ in mean square as $t \to \infty$ or as $t _ { 0 } \to - \infty$ , the right hand side of (18) also tends to 0, i.e.,

$$
\mathbf { E } \big [ | \ell \big ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \big ) - \ell \big ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t | t _ { 0 } ) \big ) | \big ]  0 .
$$

as $t \to \infty \mathrm { o r } t _ { 0 } \to - \infty .$

If ℓ is quadratic, then

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { | \ell ( { \mathbf y } ( t ) , { \mathbf y } _ { \theta } ( t ) ) - \ell ( { \mathbf y } ( t ) , { \mathbf y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) ) | = \left| \| { \mathbf y } ( t ) - { \mathbf y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } - \| { \mathbf y } ( t ) - { \mathbf y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } \right| = } \\ & { | 2 { \mathbf y } ^ { T } ( t ) ( - { \mathbf y } _ { \theta } ( t ) + { \mathbf y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) ) + \left( \| { \mathbf y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } - \| { \mathbf y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } \right) | \le } \\ & { 2 \| { \mathbf y } ^ { T } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } \| { \mathbf y } _ { \theta } ( t ) - { \mathbf y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \| _ { 2 } + | \| { \mathbf y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } - \| { \mathbf y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \| _ { 2 } | \left( \| { \mathbf y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } + \| { \mathbf y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \| _ { 2 } \right) } \end{array}
$$

Hence, using the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathbf { E } \left[ | \ell \big ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \big ) - \ell \big ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \big ) | \right] \leq } \\ & { 2 \mathbf { E } \left[ \| \mathbf { y } ^ { T } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) - \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \| _ { 2 } \right] + \mathbf { E } \left[ | \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } - \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \| _ { 2 } | \left( \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } + \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \| _ { 2 } \right) \right] \leq } \\ & { 2 \sqrt { \mathbf { E } \left[ \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) - \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } \right] } \sqrt { \mathbf { E } \left[ \| \mathbf { y } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } \right] } + } \\ & { \sqrt { \mathbf { E } \left[ \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) - \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } \right] } \sqrt { ( \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } ] + 2 \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \| _ { 2 } ] + \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } ] ) } } \end{array}
$$

Note that E $\left\lceil \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) - \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } \right\rceil \to 0$ as $t _ { 0 } \to - \infty \mathrm { o r } t \to \infty$ , since ${ \bf y } _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) - { \bf y } _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } )  0$ in the mean-square sense as $t _ { 0 } \to - \infty \mathrm { o r } t \to \infty$ . Moreover,

$$
2 \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t | t _ { 0 } ) \| _ { 2 } ] \leq \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } ] + \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t | t _ { 0 } ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } ] ,
$$

and $\mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } ]$ is constant due to stationarity of $\mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t )$ . Now for sufficiently large $t > T _ { \epsilon }$ or $- t _ { 0 } > \overleftarrow { T } _ { \epsilon } , \mathbf { E } \left[ \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) - \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } \right] < \epsilon ^ { 2 }$ , and it follows that

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } ] \leq \mathbf { E } [ ( \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } + \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) - \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ) ^ { 2 } ] } \\ & { \qquad \leq 2 ( \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } ] + \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) - \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } ] ) < 2 \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } ] + 2 \epsilon ^ { 2 } } \end{array}
$$

Hence, it follows that for $t > T _ { \epsilon } \mathrm { o r } - t _ { 0 } > T _ { \epsilon }$

$$
\sqrt  ( \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } ] + 2 \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ] \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \| _ { 2 } ] + \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } ] ) \le \sqrt { 6 \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } ] + 4 \epsilon ^ { 2 } }
$$

and $\sqrt { 6 { \bf E } [ \| { \bf y } _ { \boldsymbol \theta } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } ] + 4 \epsilon ^ { 2 } } = C$ does not depend on t. Hence, i $\mathrm { ~ f ~ } t > T _ { \epsilon } \mathrm { ~ o r ~ } { - t _ { 0 } } > T _ { \epsilon }$ , then

$$
\mathbf { E } \big [ \big | \ell \big ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \big ) - \ell \big ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t | t _ { 0 } ) \big ) \big | \big ] \leq \epsilon ( 2 \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { y } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } ] + C )
$$

from which it follows that $\mathbf { E } \big [ | \ell \big ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \big ) - \ell \big ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t | t _ { 0 } ) \big ) | \big ]  0$ as either $t _ { 0 }  - \infty$ or $t \to \infty$

That is, in all cases,

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { t \to \infty } E \bigl [ \ell \bigl ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \mathbf { y } _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ( t \mid 0 ) \bigr ) \bigr ] = E \bigl [ \ell \bigl ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \mathbf { y } _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ( t ) \bigr ) \bigr ] = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { t _ { 0 } \to - \infty } E \bigl [ \ell \bigl ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \mathbf { y } _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \bigr ) \bigr ]\tag{19}
$$

proving the lemma since $\mathcal { L } ( \boldsymbol { \theta } )$ is the right hand side of (19) by definition.

## B Proofs of Section 2.3

Remark B.1 (α as Markov-parameters). If α is the sequence of Markov parameters of a stable deterministic LTI system determined by matrices $( A , \dot { B , C } , D ) , \dot { i } . e . , \alpha ( 0 ) \stackrel { \cdot } { = } D , \alpha ( k ) = \stackrel { \cdot } { C } A ^ { k - 1 } B$ $k \geq 1$ , then the $\ell _ { 1 }$ norm $\| \alpha \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }$ and the WDMC $\theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha )$ are both finite. In this case, $\| \alpha \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }$ is the classical $\mathbf { \ell } \ell _ { 1 }$ norm ofthe underlying LTI system. Moreover, $i f M > 0 , \gamma \in [ 0 , 1 )$ are such that $\| A ^ { k } \| _ { 2 } <$ $M \gamma ^ { k }$ , then $\| \alpha \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \le \| D \| _ { 2 } + M \| B \| _ { 2 } \| C \| _ { 2 } / ( 1 - \gamma )$ and $\theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha ) \leq \| D \| + \gamma M \| B \| _ { 2 } \| C \| _ { 2 } / ( 1 - \gamma ) ^ { 2 }$ In turn, such M always exist if A is a Schur matrix, and γ can be chosen to be the spectral radius of A. In particular, $\| \alpha \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \theta _ { \infty } ( \dot { \alpha } )$ decrease as the spectral radius ofA decreases.

Finally, as promised before Lemma B.3, we can state that all the constants from Definition 2.6 are well-defined.

Lemma B.2. IfAssumption 2.2 and 2.1 hold, then the quantities $\| \alpha _ { \theta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }$ and $\theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha _ { \theta } ) , \| \bar { \alpha } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \theta _ { \infty } ( \bar { \alpha } )$ $G _ { e } ( \theta ) , G _ { e , 1 } ( \theta ) , G _ { e } , \dot { G } _ { e , 1 }$ are well defined real numbers.

Proof. From Remark B.1 and the assumption that $A _ { g }$ is Schur it follows that $\| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }$ and $\theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha _ { g } )$ are well-defined. By Assumption 2.1 there exists constants $M , \gamma , C$ such that for all $\theta \in \Theta , \| A _ { \theta } ^ { k } \| _ { 2 } \leq$ $M \gamma ^ { k } , \| B _ { \theta } \| _ { 2 } \leq C$ , and $\| C _ { \theta } \| _ { 2 } \leq C$ . Therefore, $\bar { \alpha } ( k ) = \operatorname* { s u p } \| \alpha _ { \theta } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } \leq M \gamma ^ { k } C ^ { 2 }$ and hence, by using the argument of Remark B.1, $\begin{array} { r } { \| \alpha _ { \theta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \leq \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \| \alpha \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \leq \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \bar { \alpha } ( k ) = \| \bar { \alpha } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \leq \frac { M C ^ { 2 } } { 1 - \gamma } < \infty } \end{array}$ and $\begin{array} { r } { \theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha _ { \theta } ) = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } k \| \alpha _ { \theta } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } \le \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } k \bar { \alpha } ( k ) = \theta _ { \infty } ( \bar { \alpha } ) \le \frac { M C ^ { 2 } } { ( 1 - \gamma ) ^ { 2 } } < \infty } \end{array}$ . Therefore, the quantities $\| \alpha _ { \theta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }$ and $\theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha _ { \theta } ) , \| \bar { \alpha } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \theta _ { \infty } ( \bar { \alpha } )$ defining $G _ { e } ( \theta ) , G _ { e , 1 } ( \theta ) , \overleftarrow { G } _ { e } , G _ { e , 1 }$ are well-defined, and hence so are $G _ { e } ( \theta ) , G _ { e , 1 } ( \theta ) , G _ { e } , G _ { e , 1 }$ □

Lemma B.3. Let $\alpha _ { g , y } ( k )$ and $\alpha _ { g , w } ( k )$ be the matrix formed by the first $n _ { \mathbf { y } }$ and the last $n _ { \mathbf { w } }$ rows of $\alpha _ { g } ( k )$ respectively, where $n _ { \mathbf { w } }$ is the number of entries of ${ \bf w } ( t )$ . Let $c _ { \theta , s } ( k ) ~ = ~$ $\begin{array} { r } { \left( \sum _ { l = 0 } ^ { \operatorname* { m i n } \{ k , s \} } \alpha _ { \theta } ( k ) \alpha _ { g , w } ( k - l ) \right) } \end{array}$ and $\begin{array} { r } { c _ { \theta } ( k ) = \sum _ { l = 0 } ^ { k } \alpha _ { \theta } ( k ) \alpha _ { g , w } ( k - l ) } \end{array}$

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \left[ \underline { { \mathbf { y } } } ( t ) \right] = \left( \left[ \alpha _ { g , y } \right] \star \mathbf { e } _ { g } \right) ( t ) , \quad \left[ \underline { { \mathbf { y } } } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \right] = \left( \left[ \alpha _ { \theta , y } \right] \star \mathbf { e } _ { g } \right) ( t ) , } \\ & { \mathbf { y } ( t ) - \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t ) = \left( ( \alpha _ { g , y } - c _ { \theta } ) \star \mathbf { e } _ { g } \right) ( t ) , \quad \mathbf { y } ( t ) - \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) = \left( ( \alpha _ { g , y } - c _ { \theta , t - t _ { 0 } } ) \star \mathbf { e } _ { g } \right) ( t ) } \end{array}\tag{20}
$$

Moreover, the constantsfrom Definition 2.6 satisfy:

$$
\operatorname* { m a x } \{ \| c _ { \theta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \| c _ { \theta , s } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \} \leq \| \alpha _ { \theta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ,\tag{21}
$$

$$
\operatorname* { m a x } \{ \left\| \left[ \begin{array} { l } { \alpha _ { g , y } } \\ { c _ { \theta } } \end{array} \right] \right\| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \left\| \left[ \begin{array} { l } { \alpha _ { g , y } } \\ { c _ { \theta , t - t _ { 0 } } } \end{array} \right] \right\| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \| \alpha _ { g , y } - c _ { \theta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \| \alpha _ { g , y } - c _ { \theta , s } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \} \leq G _ { e } ( \theta ) \leq G _ { e }\tag{22}
$$

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \operatorname* { m a x } \{ \theta _ { \infty } \left( \left[ \begin{array} { c } { \alpha _ { g , y } } \\ { c _ { \theta } } \end{array} \right] \right) , \theta _ { \infty } \left( \left[ \begin{array} { c } { \alpha _ { g , y } } \\ { c _ { \theta , t - t _ { 0 } } } \end{array} \right] \right) , \theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha _ { g , y } - c _ { \theta } ) , \theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha _ { g , y } - c _ { \theta , s } ) \} \leq G _ { e , 1 } ( \theta ) } \end{array}\tag{23}
$$

$$
\sum _ { t = t _ { 0 } } ^ { N + t _ { 0 } } \| c _ { \theta , t - t _ { 0 } } - c _ { \theta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \leq \theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha _ { \theta } ) \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }\tag{24}
$$

In particular, the two LTI filters in (20) are stationary.

ProofofLemma B.3. Let $C _ { w }$ denote the matrix formed by the last $n _ { w }$ rows of $C _ { g }$ . From Assumption 2.2 and the proof of Lemma 2.4 we get that

$$
\mathbf { w } ( t ) = \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { \infty } C _ { w } A _ { g } ^ { k - 1 } K _ { g } \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t - k ) + \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \alpha _ { g , w } ( k ) \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t - k ) = ( \alpha _ { g , w } \star \mathbf { e } _ { g } ) ( t )
$$

$$
\mathbf { y } ( t ) = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \alpha _ { g , y } ( k ) \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t - k )
$$

$$
\hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) = \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { t - t _ { 0 } } \hat { C } _ { \theta } \hat { A } _ { \theta } ^ { k - 1 } \hat { B } _ { \theta } \mathbf { w } ( t - k ) + \hat { D } _ { \theta } \mathbf { w } ( t ) = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { t - t _ { 0 } } \alpha _ { \theta } ( k ) \mathbf { w } ( t - k )
$$

$$
\hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t ) = \operatorname* { l i m } _ { t _ { 0 }  - \infty } \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t  { | } t _ { 0 } ) = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \alpha _ { \theta } ( k ) \mathbf { w } ( t - k )
$$

It then follows using the new index $n = k + j \geq 0$ and the standard argument involving Cauchy products of series,

$$
\begin{array} { l } { { \displaystyle \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { t - t _ { 0 } } \alpha _ { \theta } ( k ) \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { \infty } \alpha _ { g , w } ( j ) \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t - k - j ) } } \\ { ~ } \\ { { \displaystyle ~ = \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { \infty } \left( \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \operatorname* { m i n } ( n , t - t _ { 0 } ) } \alpha _ { \theta } ( k ) \alpha _ { g , w } ( n - k ) \right) \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t - n ) } } \\ { { \displaystyle ~ = \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { \infty } c _ { \theta , t - t _ { 0 } } ( n ) \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t - n ) = ( c _ { \theta , t - t _ { 0 } } \star \mathbf { e } _ { g } ) ( t ) } } \end{array}
$$

Notice that since $A _ { g }$ and $A _ { \theta }$ are Schur, the series $\scriptstyle \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \alpha _ { \theta } ( k )$ and $\scriptstyle \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \alpha _ { g , w } ( k )$ are absolutely convergent. With a similar argument, using that absolutely convergent series can be rearranged, it follows that

$$
\begin{array} { l } { { \displaystyle { \hat { \bf y } } _ { \theta } ( t ) = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \alpha _ { \theta } ( k ) \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { \infty } \alpha _ { g , w } ( j ) { \bf e } _ { g } ( t - k - j ) } \ ~ } \\ { { \displaystyle ~ = \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { \infty } \left( \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { n } \alpha _ { \theta } ( k ) \alpha _ { g , w } ( n - k ) \right) { \bf e } _ { g } ( t - n ) } \ ~ } \\ { { \displaystyle ~ = \sum _ { n = 0 } ^ { \infty } c _ { \theta } ( n ) { \bf e } _ { g } ( t - n ) = ( c _ { \theta } \star { \bf e } _ { g } ) ( t ) } } \end{array}
$$

From this the first claim of the lemma, i.e., (20), follows. The first inequality of the second claim, i.e., (21), follows by first noticing that

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \| c _ { \theta } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } \leq \displaystyle \sum _ { i = 0 } ^ { k } \| \alpha _ { \theta } ( i ) \| _ { 2 } \| \alpha _ { g , w } ( k - i ) \| _ { 2 } , } \\ & { \| c _ { \theta , t - t _ { 0 } } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } \leq \displaystyle \sum _ { i = 0 } ^ { \operatorname* { m i n } ( k , t - t _ { 0 } ) } \| \alpha _ { \theta } ( i ) \| _ { 2 } \| \alpha _ { g , w } ( k - i ) \| _ { 2 } \leq \displaystyle \sum _ { i = 0 } ^ { k } \| \alpha _ { \theta } ( i ) \| _ { 2 } \| \alpha _ { g , w } ( k - i ) \| _ { 2 } } \end{array}
$$

Since $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { i = 0 } ^ { k } | | \alpha _ { \theta } ( i ) | | _ { 2 } | | \alpha _ { g , w } ( k - i ) | | _ { 2 } } \end{array}$ is the k’th coefficient of the Cauchy-product of $\scriptstyle \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \| \alpha _ { \theta } ( k ) \| .$ 2 and $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \| \alpha _ { g , w } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } } \end{array}$ , we obtain (21) by

$$
\| c \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \| c ( k ) \| _ { 2 } \leq \sum _ { i = 0 } ^ { \infty } \| \alpha _ { \theta } ( i ) \| _ { 2 } \sum _ { j = 0 } ^ { \infty } \| \alpha _ { g , w } ( j ) \| _ { 2 } = \| \alpha _ { \theta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \| \alpha _ { g , w } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \leq \| \alpha _ { \theta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }\tag{25}
$$

with c denoting either $c _ { \theta }$ or $^ { c _ { \theta , t - t _ { 0 } } }$ . Concerning the second inequality (22), notice that

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \left\| \left[ \alpha _ { g , y } ( k ) \right] \right\| _ { 2 } \leq \| \alpha _ { g , y } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } + \| c _ { \theta } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } , \quad \| \alpha _ { g , y } ( k ) - c _ { \theta } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } \leq \| \alpha _ { g , y } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } + \| c _ { \theta } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } } \end{array}\tag{26a}
$$

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \left\| \left[ \{ \underset { { \boldsymbol { \alpha } } _ { \theta , t - t _ { 0 } } } { \boldsymbol { \alpha } } ( k ) \right] \right\| _ { 2 } \leq \| { \boldsymbol { \alpha } } _ { g , y } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } + \| { \boldsymbol { c } } _ { \theta , t - t _ { 0 } } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } , } \end{array}\tag{26b}
$$

$$
\| { \boldsymbol { \alpha } } _ { g , y } ( k ) - { \boldsymbol { c } } _ { \theta , t - t _ { 0 } } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } \leq \| { \boldsymbol { \alpha } } _ { g , y } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } + \| { \boldsymbol { c } } _ { \theta , t - t _ { 0 } } ( k ) \| _ { 2 }\tag{26c}
$$

and hence,

$$
\operatorname* { m a x } \left\{ \left\| \left[ { \alpha } _ { g , y } ^ { } \right] \right\| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \| { \alpha } _ { g , y } - { c } _ { \theta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \right\} \leq \| { \alpha } _ { g , y } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } + \| { c } _ { \theta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }\tag{27a}
$$

$$
\operatorname* { m a x } \left\{ \left\| \left[ \begin{array} { l } { \alpha _ { g , y } } \\ { c _ { \theta , t - t _ { 0 } } } \end{array} \right] \right\| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \| \alpha _ { g , y } - c _ { \theta , t - t _ { 0 } } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \right\} \leq \| \alpha _ { g , y } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } + \| c _ { \theta , t - t _ { 0 } } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }\tag{27b}
$$

From (27) and (25), the inequality of (22) follows by using the definition of $G _ { e } ( \theta )$ and the fact that max $\{ \| { \alpha } _ { g , y } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \| { \alpha } _ { g , w } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \} \le \| { \alpha } _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }$
