the last inequality (24) follows. Finally, the stationarity of the two LTI filters is a concequence of the standard result that stable LTI filters maps stationary process to stationary process, see e.g., [16, Section 5.6].

## C Proof of Theorem 3.1

The proof of Theorem 3.1 is organized as follows. First, we define an auxiliary quantity called the infinite past empirical error

$$
V _ { N } ( \theta ) = \frac { 1 } { N } \sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \ell ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t ) )
$$

It follows that $V _ { N } ( \theta )$ is an unbiased estimate of ${ \mathcal { L } } ( \theta ) , \operatorname { i . e . , } \mathbf { E } [ V _ { N } ( \theta ) ] = { \mathcal { L } } ( \theta )$

As the first step, we show the following general theorem.

Theorem C.1. Given a parameter set Θ, a prior distribution π over $\Theta , \delta \in [ 0 , 0 . 5 ) , \lambda > 0$ and $\epsilon \in \{ - 1 , 1 \}$ . Then with probability at least $1 - 2 \delta ,$ , thefollowing holds

$$
\forall \rho \in \mathcal { M } _ { \pi } : \epsilon E _ { \theta \sim \rho } \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) \leq \epsilon E _ { \theta \sim \rho } \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) + \tilde { \mathbb { B } } _ { N } ( \rho , \pi , \lambda , \epsilon )\tag{29}
$$

$$
\tilde { \mathbb { B } } _ { N } ( \rho , \pi , \lambda , \epsilon ) \triangleq \frac { 1 } { \lambda } \left( \mathrm { K L } ( \rho | | \pi ) + \ln \frac { 1 } { \delta } + \Psi ( \lambda , \pi , N , \epsilon ) \right)\tag{30}
$$

$$
\Psi ( \lambda , \pi , N , \epsilon ) \triangleq \frac { 1 } { 2 } \Big ( \ln E _ { \theta \sim \pi } \mathbf { E } [ e ^ { 2 \epsilon \lambda ( V _ { N } ( \theta ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) ) } ]
$$

$$
+ \ln E _ { \theta \sim \pi } \mathbf { E } [ e ^ { 2 \epsilon \lambda \left( \mathcal { L } \left( \theta \right) - V _ { N } \left( \theta \right) \right) } ] \biggr ) ,\tag{31}
$$

with $\begin{array} { r } { \mathrm { K L } ( \rho | | \pi ) = E _ { \theta \sim \rho } \ln \frac { \rho ( \theta ) } { \pi ( \theta ) } } \end{array}$ the Kullback–Leibler divergence.

In the theorem above, the moment generating functions $\mathbf { E } [ e ^ { 2 \epsilon \lambda ( \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) - V _ { N } ( \theta ) ) } ]$ and $\mathbf { E } [ e ^ { 2 \epsilon \lambda ( V _ { N } ( \theta ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) ) } ]$ in the right-hand side are interpreted as ∞ whenever 2ϵλ falls outside of their domain of definition. In this case, $\Psi ( \lambda , \pi , N , \epsilon )$ is taken to mean ∞ and the bounds are trivially true.

ProofofTheorem C.1. By [15, Theorem 3], for any measurable functions $X ( \theta , \omega ) , Y ( \theta , \omega ) , \tilde { \lambda } > 0$ and $\bar { \epsilon } \in \{ - 1 , 1 \}$ , with probability at least $1 - \delta$ we have:

$$
\forall \rho ~ \in ~ { \mathcal { M } } _ { \pi } ~ : ~ E _ { \theta \sim \rho } X ( \theta , \omega ) ~ \leq ~ E _ { \theta \sim \rho } Y ( \theta , \omega ) + { \frac { 1 } { \tilde { \lambda } } } \left( \mathrm { K L } ( \rho \| \pi ) + \ln { \frac { 1 } { \delta } } + \Psi _ { X , Y } ( \tilde { \lambda } ) \right) ,\tag{32}
$$

with $\Psi _ { X , Y } ( \tilde { \lambda } ) = \ln E _ { \theta \sim \pi } \mathbf { E } \big [ e ^ { \tilde { \lambda } ( X ( \theta , \omega ) - Y ( \theta , \omega ) ) } \big ]$ . Now apply the above theorem for $X = \epsilon \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) , Y =$ $\epsilon V _ { N } ( \theta )$ , and $X = \epsilon V _ { N } ( \theta ) , Y = \epsilon \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta )$ . Then by applying a union bound on the two obtained probabilistic inequalities, we obtain with probability at least $\bar { 1 } - 2 \delta$

$$
\begin{array} { l } { \displaystyle \forall \rho \in { \mathcal { M } } _ { \pi } : \epsilon E _ { \theta \sim \rho } \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) \leq \epsilon E _ { \theta \sim \rho } \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) } \\ { \displaystyle \qquad + \frac { 1 } { \tilde { \lambda } } \left( 2 \mathrm { K L } ( \rho \| \pi ) + 2 \ln \frac { 1 } { \delta } + { \Psi } _ { \epsilon V _ { N } , \epsilon \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } } ( \tilde { \lambda } ) + { \Psi } _ { \epsilon \mathcal { L } , \epsilon V _ { N } } ( \tilde { \lambda } ) \right) } \end{array}
$$

With some algebraic manipulation and $\tilde { \lambda } = 2 \lambda$ , we obtain the statement of the theorem.

For the case of bounded innovation noise or Lipschitz loss function, we then show that $\mathbf { E } [ e ^ { 2 \lambda ( V _ { N } ( \theta ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) ) } ]$ and $\mathbf { E } [ e ^ { 2 \lambda ( \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) - V _ { N } ( \theta ) ) } ]$ ] can be both upper bounded by $\mathcal { C } _ { i } ( \theta , \lambda , N , \delta , \epsilon )$ , from which the statement of the theorem follows. For the case of quadaratic loss function, we will show that it can be reduced to the case of Lipschitz loss function with probability $\delta / 3$ and then we apply the result for Lipschitz loss with $\delta / 3$ instead of δ.

## C.1 Proof of Theorem 3.1: bounded case

For the sake of readability, we repeat below the basic assumptions for the case of bounded data.

Assumption C.2 (Bounded noise and Lipschitz loss). The innovation noise $\mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) f r o m \ : ( 4 )$ is essentially bounded , i.e.,

$$
\| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { \infty } \leq c _ { \mathbf { e } } , \forall t \in \mathbb { Z }\tag{33}
$$

and the lossfunction ℓ is either $L _ { \ell } { - } L i p s c h i t z$ or quadratic.

The following Lemma is obvious in the $L _ { \ell } { - } \mathbf { I }$ Lipschits case, and follow by applying Cauchy–Schwarz in the quadratic case.

Lemma C.3. Let $\begin{array} { r c l r c l r c l } { B } & { = } & { \{ ( z , z ^ { \prime } ) } & { \in } & { \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { \mathbf { y } } } \times \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { \mathbf { y } } } } & { \vert } & { \Vert z \mathrm { ~ - ~ } z ^ { \prime } \Vert _ { 2 } } & { \le } & { G _ { e } ( \theta ) \sqrt { n _ { \mathbf { e } } } c _ { \mathbf { e } } \} } \end{array}$ . Then $( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) ) , ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid 0 ) ) \in B .$ for all t $, t _ { 0 } \leq t ,$ and for all $( z _ { 1 } , z _ { 2 } ) , ( z _ { 3 } , z _ { 4 } ) \in \dot { B } ,$

$$
\ell ( z _ { 1 } , z _ { 2 } ) - \ell ( z _ { 3 } , z _ { 4 } ) \leq L ( \theta ) \left( \| z _ { 1 } - z _ { 3 } \| _ { 2 } + \| z _ { 2 } - z _ { 4 } \| _ { 2 } \right)
$$

where $L ( \theta )$ is as in Theorem 3.1, i.e., $L ( \theta ) = 2 G _ { e } ( \theta ) \sqrt { n _ { \mathbf { e } } } c _ { \mathbf { e } }$ ifℓ-quadratic, and $L ( \theta ) = L _ { \ell } i f \ell$ is $L _ { \ell } { - } L i p s c h i t z .$

Remark C.4 (Norm loss $L _ { \ell ^ { - } } \mathbf { I }$ ipschitz). Note that if the loss ℓ is the p norm of the difference, i.e., $\ell ( y , \hat { y } ) = \| y - \hat { y } \| _ { p } ,$ then $\ell ( y , \hat { y } ) \leq C _ { p } \| y - \hat { y } \| _ { 2 }$ with $C _ { p } = \sqrt { n _ { \mathbf { y } } } i f p = 1$ , and $C _ { p } = 1 ~ i f p = \infty ~ o r$ $p = 2 .$

Lemma C.5. Let assumptions 2.2, 2.1 and C.2 hold. Then

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathbf { E } [ e ^ { \left( \epsilon \lambda \vert V _ { N } ( \theta ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) \vert \right) } ] \leq e ^ { \left( \frac { L ( \theta ) \lambda } { N } \theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha _ { \theta } ) \Vert \alpha _ { g } \Vert _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ( c _ { \mathbf { e } } \sqrt { n _ { \mathbf { e } } } ) \right) } = e ^ { C _ { b , 1 } ( \lambda , \theta , L ( \theta ) , c _ { \mathbf { e } } , N ) } } \end{array}
$$

ProofC.5 . Using the Lipschitz property of the loss function it follows that

$$
\epsilon ( V _ { N } ( \theta ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) ) \leq | V _ { N } ( \theta ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) | \leq \frac { 1 } { N } \sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } | \ell ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t ) ) - \ell ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t \mid 0 ) ) | \leq \frac { 1 } { N } \sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } | \ell ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t ) ) - \ell ( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t \mid 0 ) ) | ,
$$

$$
{ \frac { L ( \theta ) } { N } } \sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \| { \hat { \mathbf { y } } } _ { \theta } ( t ) - { \hat { \mathbf { y } } } _ { \theta } ( t \mid 0 ) \| _ { 2 }\tag{34}
$$

From Lemma B.3 it follows that

$$
{ \hat { \mathbf { y } } } _ { \theta } ( t ) - { \hat { \mathbf { y } } } _ { \theta } ( t \mid 0 ) = ( ( c _ { \theta } - c _ { \theta , t } ) \star \mathbf { e } _ { g } ) ( t ) = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } ( c _ { \theta } - c _ { \theta , t } ) ( k ) \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t - k )
$$

and hence

$$
\sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \| \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t ) - \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t \mid 0 ) \| _ { 2 } \leq \sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \| ( c _ { \theta } - c _ { \theta , t } ) ( k ) \| _ { 2 } \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t - k ) \| _ { 2 } \leq \sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \| c _ { \theta } - c _ { \theta , t } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \sqrt { n _ { \mathrm { e } } } c _ { \mathrm { e } }
$$

From (24) it follows that

$$
\sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \| c _ { \theta } - c _ { \theta , t } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \leq \theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha _ { \theta } ) \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }
$$

and hence,

$$
\sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \| \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ( t ) - \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ( t \mid 0 ) \| _ { 2 } \leq \theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ) \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \sqrt { n _ { \mathbf { e } } } c _ { \mathbf { e } }
$$

from which the statement of the lemma follows using the definition of $\mathcal { C } _ { b , 1 } ( \lambda , \theta , L ( \theta ) , c _ { \bf e } , N )$ □

Lemma C.6. Let assumptions 2.2, 2.1 and C.2 hold. Then

$$
\mathbf { E } [ e ^ { \epsilon \lambda ( \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) - V _ { N } ( \theta ) ) } ] \leq e ^ { \frac { ( L ( \theta ) ) ^ { 2 } \lambda ^ { 2 } } { 2 N } ( G _ { \epsilon } ( \theta ) + 2 G _ { \epsilon , 1 } ( \theta ) ) ( c _ { \circ } \sqrt { n _ { \mathrm { e } } } ) ^ { 2 } } = e ^ { \mathcal { C } _ { b , 2 } ( \lambda , \theta , L ( \theta ) , c _ { \circ } , N ) }\tag{35}
$$

ProofofLemma C.6 . For each $\Sigma ( \theta ) \in { \mathcal { F } } _ { : }$ , consider $\mathbf { z } _ { t } ~ = ~ \left\lceil \mathbf { y } ^ { T } ( t ) ~ \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ^ { T } ( t ) \right\rceil ^ { T }$ . Then by Lemma $\mathbf { B } . 3 , \mathbf { z } _ { t } = \left( \left[ { \alpha } _ { g , y } \right] \star \mathbf { e } _ { g } \right) ( t )$ and $\mathbf { z } _ { t }$ is stationary. Moreover, as $\left\| \left[ { \alpha } _ { g , y } \right] \right\| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \le G _ { e } ( \theta ) < \infty$ and $\theta _ { \infty } \left( \left[ \begin{array} { c } { { \alpha _ { g , y } } } \\ { { c _ { \theta } } } \end{array} \right] \right) \le G _ { e , 1 } ( \theta ) < \infty$ , it follows that $\mathbf { z } _ { t }$ is a Bernoulli-shift. In particular, $\| \mathbf { z } _ { t } \| _ { \infty } \leq$ $G _ { e } ( \theta ) \overline { { c } } _ { \bf e } \sqrt { n _ { \bf e } } ,$ and by [1, Proposition 4.2] $\mathbf { z } _ { t }$ is a weakly dependent process in the terminology of [1], with $\| \mathbf { z } _ { 0 } \| _ { \infty } \leq G _ { e } ( \theta ) c _ { \mathbf { e } } \sqrt { n _ { \mathbf { e } } } .$ , and the mixing coefficient $\theta _ { \infty , N } ( 1 )$ of $\mathbf { z } _ { t }$ satisfies $\theta _ { \infty , N } ( \bar { 1 } ) <$ $2 G _ { e , 1 } ( \theta ) c _ { \mathbf { e } } \sqrt { n _ { \mathbf { e } } } .$

If ℓ is L<sub>ℓ</sub>-Lipschitz, then let $B ~ = ~ \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { \mathbf { y } } } ~ \times ~ \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { \mathbf { y } } }$ . If ℓ is quadratic, then consider the set B from Lemma C.3. Define the function $\begin{array} { r } { h ( z _ { 0 } , z _ { 0 } ^ { \prime } , \ldots , z _ { N - 1 } , z _ { N - 1 } ^ { \prime } ) ~ = ~ \epsilon \frac { 1 } { L ( \theta ) } \sum _ { i = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \ell ( z _ { i } , z _ { i } ^ { \prime } ) } \end{array}$ $z _ { 0 } , \dotsc , z _ { N - 1 } , z _ { 0 } ^ { \prime } , \dotsc , z _ { N - 1 } ^ { \prime } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { \mathbf { y } } } \mathrm { o n } B ^ { N }$ . We claim that h has Lipschitz constant 1 in the following sense:

$$
| h ( z _ { 0 } , z _ { 0 } ^ { \prime } , \ldots , z _ { N - 1 } , z _ { N - 1 } ^ { \prime } ) - h ( \tilde { z } _ { 0 } , \tilde { z } _ { 0 } ^ { \prime } , \ldots , \tilde { z } _ { N - 1 } , \tilde { z } _ { N - 1 } ^ { \prime } ) | \leq \sum _ { i = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \| z _ { i } - \tilde { z } _ { i } \| _ { 2 } + \| z _ { i } ^ { \prime } - \tilde { z } _ { i } ^ { \prime } \| _ { 2 }
$$

Indeed, using Lemma C.3,

$$
\begin{array} { l } { \displaystyle | h ( z _ { 0 } , z _ { 0 } ^ { \prime } , \ldots , z _ { N - 1 } , z _ { N - 1 } ^ { \prime } ) - h ( \tilde { z } _ { 0 } , \tilde { z } _ { 0 } ^ { \prime } , \ldots , \tilde { z } _ { N - 1 } , \tilde { z } _ { N - 1 } ^ { \prime } ) | = \left| \frac { \epsilon } { L ( \theta ) } \sum _ { i = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \ell ( z _ { i } , z _ { i } ^ { \prime } ) - \ell ( \tilde { z } _ { i } , \tilde { z } _ { i } ^ { \prime } ) \right| } \\ { \displaystyle \leq \frac { 1 } { L ( \theta ) } \sum _ { i = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } L ( \theta ) ( \| z _ { i } - \tilde { z } _ { i } \| _ { 2 } + \| z _ { i } ^ { \prime } - \tilde { z } _ { i } ^ { \prime } \| _ { 2 } ) = \sum _ { i = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \| z _ { i } - \tilde { z } _ { i } \| _ { 2 } + \| z _ { i } ^ { \prime } - \tilde { z } _ { i } ^ { \prime } \| _ { 2 } } \end{array}
$$

If ℓ is $L _ { \ell ^ { - 1 } }$ ipschitz, then h is already defined on $( \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { \mathbf { y } } } \times \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { \mathbf { y } } } ) ^ { N }$ . If ℓ is quadratic, we can use Kirszbraun’s theorem for scalar valued functions [13, 2.10.44,page 202], from which it then follows that h can be extended to a 1-Lipschitz map on $( \bar { \mathbb { R } } ^ { n _ { \mathbf { y } } } \times \mathbb { R } ^ { n _ { \mathbf { y } } } ) ^ { \hat { N } }$ without changing its values on $B ^ { N }$ In fact, such an extension can be defined as $\begin{array} { r } { h _ { e x t } ( z ) = \operatorname* { i n f } _ { x \in B ^ { N } } \{ h ( x ) + \| z - \overline { { x } } \| _ { 2 } \} } \end{array}$ . By abuse of notation this extension will be denote by h too. Notice that by Lemma C.3, $( \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \mathbf { y } _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ( t ) ) \in B$ for all t, hence $\begin{array} { r } { h ( \mathbf { y } ( 0 ) , \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( 0 ) , \dots , \mathbf { y } ( N - 1 ) , \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( N - 1 ) ) = \frac { \epsilon } { L ( \theta ) } \sum _ { i = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \ell ( \mathbf { y } ( i ) , \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( i ) ) = \frac { \epsilon } { L ( \theta ) } N V _ { N } ( \theta ) } \end{array}$

Notice that,

$$
\epsilon \lambda ( { \bf E } [ V _ { N } ( \theta ) ] - V _ { N } ( \theta ) ) = \frac { \lambda L ( \theta ) } { N } \left( { \bf E } [ h ] - h \right) .
$$

Now we can apply [1, Theorem 6.6], from which it follows that for any $q \geq 0$

$$
\mathbf { E } [ e ^ { q ( \mathbf { E } [ h ] - h ) } ] \leq e ^ { \frac { q ^ { 2 } N } { 2 } ( \| \mathbf { z } _ { t } \| _ { \infty } + \theta _ { \infty , N } ( 1 ) ) ^ { 2 } }\tag{36}
$$

By choosing $\begin{array} { r } { q = \frac { \lambda L ( \theta ) } { N } } \end{array}$ and using the definition of $\mathcal { C } _ { b , 2 } ( \lambda , \theta , L ( \theta ) , c _ { \bf e } , N )$ , we obtain the statement of the lemma . □

Proof of Theorem 3.1: bounded processes. The statement of the theorem follows from Theorem C.1 by replacing $\mathbf { E } [ e ^ { 2 \lambda \epsilon ( V _ { N } ( \theta ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) ) } ]$ and ${ \bf E } [ e ^ { 2 \lambda \epsilon ( \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) - V _ { N } ( \theta ) ) } ]$ with their upper bound from Lemma C.5 and Lemma C.6 respectively. □

## C.2 Proof of Theorem 3.1: unbounded processes and Lipschitz loss functions

Recall that according to our assumptions, the 2-norm of the noise process $\mathbf { e } _ { g }$ is subgaussian. Bounded processes have this property, however, in this section we will not assume that the process $\mathbf { e } _ { g }$ is bounded.

Recall that we also consider Lipschitz losses.

Assumption C.7 (Lipschitz loss). We assume that the loss function ℓ is L<sub>ℓ</sub>-Lipschitz, i.e., for all $y , \hat { y } , \hat { y ^ { \prime } } , \hat { y ^ { \prime } } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n } \mathbf { y } , | \ell ( \hat { y } , \hat { y } ) - \ell ( y ^ { \prime } , \hat { y } ^ { \prime } ) | \leq L _ { \ell } ( \| y - y ^ { \prime } \| _ { 2 } + \| \hat { y } - \hat { y } ^ { \prime } \| _ { 2 } )$

The main idea is to use Theorem C.1 and to derive bounds for moment generating functions of $\mathbf { E } [ 2 \lambda \epsilon ( V _ { N } ( \theta ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) ) ]$ and $\mathbf { E } [ 2 \lambda \epsilon ( { \mathcal { L } } ( \theta ) - V _ { N } ( \theta ) ) ]$ . In order to achieve this, we will reduce the problem of bounding $\mathbf { E } [ 2 \lambda \epsilon ( V _ { N } ( \theta ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) ) ]$ ] and $\mathbf { E } [ 2 \lambda \epsilon ( { \mathcal { L } } ( \theta ) - V _ { N } ( \theta ) ) ]$ to the same problem for bounded noises, by truncating the unbounded noise $\mathbf { e } _ { g }$ which drives the data generator. This problem was solved in Lemma C.5 and Lemma $\mathrm { C } . 6 ,$ which thus can be re-used.

To this end, let $C > 0$ be any constant and let $\bar { \mathbf { e } } _ { g } ( t )$ be the truncated version of $\mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) , \mathrm { i . e . }$ •,

$$
\bar { \mathbf { e } } _ { g } ( t ) = \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \chi _ { \{ \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| < C \} } ( t ) : = \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \chi ( \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| < C ) .\tag{37}
$$

Let $\bar { \bf w } ( t ) = \left[ \bar { \bf y } ^ { T } ( t ) \right]$ be the output of the data generator, if ${ \bf e } _ { g } ( t )$ is replaced by $\bar { \mathbf { e } } _ { g } ( t )$ , and let $\hat { \bar { \mathbf { y } } } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } )$ be the output of the predictor (1) parametrized by θ with w replaced by $\bar { \bf w } ,$ and let $\hat { \bar { \mathbf { y } } } _ { \theta } ( t )$ be the limit lim ${ \mathbf { \psi } } _ { { \mathbf { l } } _ { t _ { 0 } }  - \infty } \hat { \bar { \mathbf { y } } } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } )$ . Note that Lemma 2.4 applies, as $\bar { \mathbf e } _ { g } ( t )$ satisfies all the assumptions of Assumption 2.2. Let $\begin{array} { r } { \bar { V } _ { N } ( \theta ) = \frac { 1 } { N } \ell ( \bar { \bf y } ( t ) , \hat { \bar { \bf y } } _ { \theta } ( t ) ) } \end{array}$ be the corresponding infinite past empirical error, $\begin{array} { r } { \hat { \bar { \mathcal { L } } } _ { N } ( \theta ) = \frac { 1 } { N } \sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \ell ( \bar { \mathbf { y } } ( t ) , \hat { \bar { \mathbf { y } } } _ { \theta } ( t \mid 0 ) ) } \end{array}$ ) the finite past empirical error, and $\bar { \mathcal { L } } ( \boldsymbol { \theta } ) = \mathbf { E } [ \ell ( \bar { \mathbf { y } } ( t ) , \hat { \bar { \mathbf { y } } } ( t ) ) ^ { \cdot }$ the true error for the model corresponding to $\theta \in \Theta$ , if it is fed with the truncated signal w¯ instead of w.

It turns out that we can bound the difference between these quantities and their non-truncated counterparts. The basic steps are as follows: First, using Lipschitz properties of the loss function, we bound the moment generating functions of the differences $\begin{array} { r } { \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \theta \in \Theta } | \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) - \hat { \bar { \mathcal { L } } } _ { N } ( \theta ) | } \end{array}$ ${ \mathrm { s u p } } _ { \theta \in \Theta } | V _ { N } ( \theta ) - { \bar { V } } _ { N } ( \theta ) |$ by the corresponding quantity for the $\ell _ { 2 }$ loss, i.e.; by the moment generating functions of $\begin{array} { r } { \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \theta \in \Theta } \sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \| \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t \mid 0 ) - \hat { \bar { \mathbf { y } } } _ { \theta } ( t \mid t _ { 0 } ) \| _ { 2 } } \end{array}$ and $\begin{array} { r } { \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \theta \in \Theta } \sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \| \mathbf { y } ( t ) - \bar { \mathbf { y } } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } } \end{array}$ Lemma C.8.

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathbf { E } [ e ^ { \lambda \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \theta \in \Theta } \vert \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) \vert } ] \leq } \\ & { \quad \sqrt { \mathbf { E } [ e ^ { \frac { 2 \lambda L _ { \ell } } { N } \sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } ( \Vert \mathbf { y } ( t ) - \bar { \mathbf { y } } ( t ) \Vert _ { 2 } } ] } \sqrt { \mathbf { E } [ e ^ { \frac { 2 \lambda L _ { \ell } } { N } \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \theta \in \Theta } \sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \Vert \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t | 0 ) - \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t | t _ { 0 } ) \Vert _ { 2 } } ] } } \end{array}\tag{38}

Proof of Theorem 3.1: unbounded noise and Lipschitz loss. Therefore, as $C \| \bar { \alpha } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } = 2 \ln ( N ) \operatorname* { m a x } \{ 1 , \| \bar { \alpha } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \}$

$$

Using Lemma C.13 with $\| \alpha _ { g , y } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }$ and $\| \boldsymbol { \alpha } _ { g , w } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }$ replaced by their upper bound $\| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }$ it follows that for any $\lambda < L _ { \ell } / 1 6 N$

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathbf { E } \big [ e ^ { 2 \lambda \epsilon ( V _ { N } ( \theta ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) ) } \big ] \leq e ^ { \bar { C } _ { u b } ( 2 \lambda , L _ { \ell } , N ) + \frac { 1 } { 2 } C _ { b , 1 } ( 4 \lambda , \theta , L _ { \ell } , \frac { 2 \ln ( N ) } { K \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } } , N ) } = e ^ { \bar { C } _ { 1 } ( 2 \lambda , \theta , N , \delta , \epsilon ) } } \\ & { \mathbf { E } \big [ e ^ { 2 \lambda \epsilon ( \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) - V _ { N } ( \theta ) ) } \big ] \leq e ^ { \bar { C } _ { u b } ( 2 \lambda , L _ { \ell } , N ) + \frac { 1 } { 2 } C _ { b , 2 } ( 4 \lambda , \theta , L _ { \ell } , \frac { 2 \ln ( N ) } { K \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } } , N ) } = e ^ { \bar { C } _ { 2 } ( 2 \lambda , \theta , N , \delta , \epsilon ) } } \end{array}\tag{44}
$$

The statement of the theorem follows from Theorem C.1 by replacing $\mathbf { E } [ e ^ { 2 \lambda \epsilon ( V _ { N } ( \theta ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) ) } ]$ and $\mathbf { E } [ e ^ { 2 \lambda \epsilon ( \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) - V _ { N } ( \theta ) ) } ]$ with their upper bound from (44).

The rest of the section is devoted to the proof of the lemmas used above.

First, we prove Lemma C.9

Proof of Lemma C.9. First note that $\mathbf { z } ( t ) : = \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } - \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ]$ is zero mean sub-Gaussian, and using sub-additivity of the sub-Gaussian norm we get $\begin{array} { r l r } { \| \bar { \bf z } ( t ) \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } } & { { } \le } & { \| \| { \bf e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } + } \end{array}$ $\| \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ] \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } ~ = ~ \| \bar { \mathbf { e } } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } + \| \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ] \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } }$ . Now from the proof of [43, Proposition $2 . 6 . 1 ]$ it follows that $\mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ] \ \leq \ 3 \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } ,$ , and by [43, Exercise 2.24], $\| \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ] \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } \leq$ $\mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ] / { \sqrt { \ln 2 } } \leq 2 \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ]$ . Hence $\| \mathbf { z } ( t ) \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } \leq 7 \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } }$

Then from the proof of [43, Proposition 2.6.1] it follows that $\mathbf { E } [ e ^ { \lambda ( \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } - \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ] ) } ] \le e ^ { \lambda ^ { 2 } K ^ { 2 } }$ and $\begin{array} { r } { K = \sqrt { \frac { 3 } { 2 } \| { \mathbf z } ( t ) \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } } \leq 1 4 \| { \mathbf e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } } \end{array}$ . It then follows that $\Psi _ { \mathbf { e } } ( \lambda ) \leq e ^ { ( \lambda ^ { 2 } K ^ { 2 } + \lambda \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ] ) }$ and the statement of the lemma follows. □

In order to present the proof of Lemma C.8-C.13, we need the following sequence of results.

Lemma C.14. Let z be positive valued random variable such thatfor all $s \leq \bar { \lambda }$ its moment generating function is well-defined. Thenfor any $C > 0$

$$
{ \bf E } [ { \bf z } \chi ( { \bf z } > C ) ] \leq \frac { C { \bf E } [ e ^ { { \bar { \lambda } } { \bf z } } ] } { e ^ { { \bar { \lambda } } C } - 1 }
$$

Note that, as in $( 3 7 )$ , we use $\chi ( \mathbf { z } > C )$ as a shorthand for the indicator function $\chi _ { \{ { \bf z } > C \} } ( { \bf z } )$ . This practices will be used in the sequel.

Proof of Lemma $C . I 4 .$ Let us consider $\begin{array} { r } { \phi ( x ) = \frac { e ^ { x } - 1 } { x } } \end{array}$ . It then follows that $\phi$ is a monotonically increasing function of x for $x > 0$ . Hence,

$$
\frac { \phi ( \bar { \lambda } { \bf z } ) } { \phi ( \bar { \lambda } C ) } \geq \chi ( { \bf z } > C ) .
$$

Indeed, if $\mathbf { z } > C ,$ , then $\begin{array} { r } { \frac { \phi ( \bar { \lambda } \mathbf { z } ) } { \phi ( \bar { \lambda } C ) } \geq 1 = \chi ( \mathbf { z } > C ) . \operatorname { I f } \mathbf { z } \leq C , } \end{array}$ , then $\begin{array} { r } { 1 > \frac { \phi ( \bar { \lambda } \mathbf { z } ) } { \phi ( \bar { \lambda } C ) } \geq 0 = \chi ( \mathbf { z } > C ) } \end{array}$ . It then follows that

$$
{ \bf z } \frac { \phi ( \bar { \lambda } { \bf z } ) } { \phi ( \bar { \lambda } C ) } \ge { \bf z } \chi ( { \bf z } > C )
$$

and by taking expectations it follows that

$$
{ \bf E } [ { \bf z } \chi ( { \bf z } > C ) ) ] \leq \frac { 1 } { \phi ( \bar { \lambda } C ) } { \bf E } [ { \bf z } \phi ( \bar { \lambda } { \bf z } ) ]
$$

Note that

$$
{ \bf z } \phi ( \bar { \lambda } { \bf z } ) = \frac { e ^ { \bar { \lambda } { \bf z } } - 1 } { \bar { \lambda } }
$$

and hence by using the inequality $e ^ { x } - 1 \leq e ^ { x }$ for $x \geq 0$ it follows that

$$
\mathbf { E } [ \mathbf { z } \phi ( \bar { \lambda } \mathbf { z } ) ] = \frac { \mathbf { E } [ e ^ { \bar { \lambda } \mathbf { z } } ] - 1 } { \bar { \lambda } } \leq \frac { \mathbf { E } [ e ^ { \bar { \lambda } \mathbf { z } } ] } { \bar { \lambda } }
$$

By noticing that

$$
\bar { \lambda } \phi ( \bar { \lambda } C ) = \frac { e ^ { \bar { \lambda } C } - 1 } { C }
$$

the statement of the lemma follows.

Lemma C.15.

$$
\mathbf { E } [ e ^ { s \| e _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } \chi ( \| { \mathbf { e } } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } > C ) } ] \leq \left( 1 + s \frac { \bar { \Psi } _ { { \mathbf { e } } _ { g } } ( \bar { \lambda } ) C } { e ^ { \bar { \lambda } C } - 1 } + s ^ { 2 } \frac { 2 \bar { \Psi } _ { { \mathbf { e } } _ { g } } ( \bar { \lambda } ) } { \bar { \lambda } ^ { 2 } } \right) : = \Psi _ { { \mathbf { e } } _ { g } } ( s , \bar { \lambda } , C )
$$

for all $s \in [ 0 , 0 . 5 \bar { \lambda } )$

Proof Lemma C.15. First note that

$$
\begin{array} { l } { \displaystyle \mathbf { E } [ e ^ { s \| { \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) } \| _ { 2 } \chi ( \| { \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) } \| _ { 2 } > C ) ) } ] = 1 + s \mathbf { E } [ \| { \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) } \| _ { 2 } \chi ( \| { \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) } \| _ { 2 } > C ) ) ] + } \\ { \displaystyle \sum _ { k = 2 } ^ { \infty } \frac { \mathbf { E } [ \| { \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) } \| _ { 2 } ^ { k } \chi ( \| { \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) } \| _ { 2 } > C ) ] s ^ { k } ] } { k ! } . } \end{array}\tag{45}
$$

and $\mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { k } \chi ( \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } > C ) \leq \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { k } ] .$

As in Lemma C.9 let $\Psi _ { { \bf e } _ { g } } ( \bar { \lambda } ) = { \bf E } [ e ^ { \bar { \lambda } \| { \bf e } _ { g } ( 0 ) \| _ { 2 } } ] = { \bf E } [ e ^ { \bar { \lambda } \| { \bf e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } } ]$ be the moment generating function for $\| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } .$ . Then

$$
\Psi _ { { \bf e } _ { g } } ( \bar { \lambda } ) = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \frac { { \bf E } [ \| { \bf e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { k } ] } { k ! } \bar { \lambda } ^ { k }
$$

and we get

$$
\frac { \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { k } ] } { k ! } \leq \frac { \Psi _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( \bar { \lambda } ) } { \bar { \lambda } ^ { k } } .
$$

It follows that for all $k \geq 2 .$

$$
\frac { \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { k } \chi ( \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } > C ) ] s ^ { k } } { k ! } \leq \frac { \mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { k } ] s ^ { k } } { k ! } \leq \Psi _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( \bar { \lambda } ) \frac { s ^ { k } } { \bar { \lambda } ^ { k } } .
$$

Therefore, for all $s \in [ 0 , 0 . 5 \bar { \lambda } )$ ).

$$
\sum _ { k = 2 } ^ { \infty } \frac { \mathbf { E } [ \| e _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { k } \chi ( \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } > C ) ] s ^ { k } ] } { k ! } \leq \sum _ { k = 2 } ^ { \infty } \Psi _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( \bar { \lambda } ) \frac { s ^ { k } } { \bar { \lambda } ^ { k } } = \Psi _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( \bar { \lambda } ) \frac { s ^ { 2 } } { \bar { \lambda } ^ { 2 } } \frac { 1 } { 1 - \frac { s } { \lambda } } =\tag{46}
$$

$$
\Psi _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( \bar { \lambda } ) \frac { s ^ { 2 } } { \bar { \lambda } ( \bar { \lambda } - s ) } \leq \Psi _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( \bar { \lambda } ) \frac { s ^ { 2 } } { 0 . 5 \bar { \lambda } ^ { 2 } } = 2 \Psi _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( \bar { \lambda } ) \frac { s ^ { 2 } } { \bar { \lambda } ^ { 2 } }
$$

where in the last inequality we used that as $s < 0 . 5 \bar { \lambda } , \bar { \lambda } - s \ge 0 . 5 \bar { \lambda }$ . Finally, use Lemma C.14 with $\mathbf { z } = \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 }$ to obtain

$$
\mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } \chi ( \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } > C ) ) ] \leq \frac { \Psi _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( \bar { \lambda } ) C } { e ^ { \bar { \lambda } C } - 1 }\tag{47}
$$

The result now follows by combining (45), (46) and (47), and then use Lemma C.9.

We will also need the following technical lemma

Lemma C.16. Let $a _ { k } \geq 0$ be a sequence of positive numbers such that the series $\textstyle \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } a _ { k }$ is convergent. For any N, define $\begin{array} { r } { c _ { k , N } = \sum _ { l = \mathrm { m a x } \{ 0 , k - N + 1 \} } ^ { k } a _ { l } } \end{array}$ . Then $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { L } c _ { k , N } \leq N \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { L } a _ { k } , } \end{array}$ , the series $\textstyle \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } c _ { k , N }$ is convergent, and $\textstyle \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } c _ { k , N } \leq N \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } a _ { k }$

Proof of Lemma C.16. We will prove by induction on $L \ge N - 1$ that

$$
\sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { L } c _ { k , N } = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { L - N + 1 } N a _ { k } + \sum _ { k = L - N + 2 } ^ { L } ( L - k + 1 ) a _ { k }\tag{48}
$$

For $L = N - 1$ , by induction on $N _ { \ast }$ , it can be shown that

$$
\sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } c _ { k , N } = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \sum _ { l = 0 } ^ { k } a _ { l } = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } ( N - k ) a _ { k } .
$$

which yield (48) for $L = N - 1$ . Assume (48) holds for $N - 1 \leq L ^ { \prime } \leq L$ . Notice that $c _ { L + 1 , N } =$ $\scriptstyle \sum _ { l = L - N + 2 } ^ { L } a _ { l }$ and hence

$$
\begin{array} { l } { { \displaystyle \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { L + 1 } c _ { k , N } = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { L } c _ { k , N } + c _ { L + 1 , N } = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { L - N + 1 } N a _ { k } + \sum _ { k = L - N + 2 } ^ { L } ( L - k + 1 ) a _ { k } + \sum _ { l = L - N + 2 } ^ { L } a _ { l } = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { L } b , k = L , } } \\ { { \displaystyle \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { L - N + 1 } N a _ { k } + \sum _ { k = L - N + 2 } ^ { L } ( L - k + 2 ) a _ { k } = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { L - N + 1 } N a _ { k } + ( L - ( L - N + 2 ) + 2 ) a _ { L - N + 2 } + \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { L - N + 1 } b , k = L , } } \\ { { \displaystyle \sum _ { k = L - N + 3 } ^ { L } ( ( L + 1 ) - k + 1 ) a _ { k } = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { L - N + 2 } N a _ { k } + \sum _ { k = L - N + 3 } ^ { L } ( ( L + 1 ) - k + 1 ) a _ { k } } } \end{array}
$$

The last expression is the right-hand side of (48) for L being replaced by $L + 1$ , thus proving (48). Note that for $k = L - \mathbf { \bar { \Phi } } N + 2 , \ldots , L$ we have $L - k + \bar { 1 } \leq \mathbf { \bar { \phi } }$ , and hence (48) implies that $\textstyle \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { L } c _ { k , N } \leq N \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { L } a _ { k }$ . Since $a _ { k }$ and $c _ { k }$ are nonnegative, and $\textstyle \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } a _ { k }$ is convergent, the rest of the lemma follows. □

Lemma C.17. Let I be an index set, and $\{ \beta _ { i , t } ( k ) \} _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } , i \in { \bf I } ,$ , be a sequence of matrices such that $\| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } < \infty$ , where $\begin{array} { r } { \bar { \beta } ( k ) = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { i \in \mathbf { I } , t \in \mathbb { Z } } \| \beta _ { i , t } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } } \end{array}$ . Let $\mathbf { z } _ { i } ( t ) = ( \beta _ { i , t } \star \mathbf { e } _ { g } ) ( t )$ and $\bar { \bf z } _ { i } ( t ) =$ $( \beta _ { i , t } \star \bar { \bf e } _ { g } ) ( t )$ . Then for $\lambda < 0 . 5 N$

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \displaystyle \mathbf { E } \big [ e ^ { \frac { \lambda } { N } \operatorname* { s u p } _ { i \in \mathbf { I } } \sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \| \mathbf { z } ( t ) - \bar { \mathbf { z } } _ { i } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } } \big ] \leq \prod _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \bar { \Psi } _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } \left( \frac { c ( k ) \lambda } { N } , \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , C \right) \leq e ^ { \mathcal { C } ( \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \lambda , N , C ) } } \\ & { \displaystyle { \mathcal { C } } ( s , \lambda , N , C ) = \lambda \frac { \bar { \Psi } _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( s ) s ^ { 2 } C } { ( e ^ { s C } - 1 ) } + \frac { \lambda ^ { 2 } } { N } 2 \bar { \Psi } _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( s ) } \end{array}
$$

where $\begin{array} { r } { c ( k ) = \sum _ { l = \mathrm { m a x } \{ 0 , k - N + 1 \} } ^ { k } \| \bar { \beta } ( l ) \| _ { 2 } } \end{array}$ and the infinite product on the right-hand side is welldefined.

Proof of Lemma C.17. Note that

$$
c ( k ) \leq \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }\tag{49}
$$

and by Lemma C.16

$$
\sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } c ( k ) \leq N \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \| \bar { \beta } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } = N \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }\tag{50}
$$

Note now that

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \displaystyle \sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \| \mathbf { z } _ { t , t } ( t ) - \bar { \mathbf { z } } _ { t , t } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } } \\ & { \displaystyle \leq \sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \| \beta _ { t , t } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t - k ) - \bar { \mathbf { e } } _ { g } ( t - k ) \| _ { 2 } } \\ & { \displaystyle \leq \sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \| \bar { \beta } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t - k ) \chi ( \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t - k ) \| _ { 2 } > C ) \| _ { 2 } } \\ & { \displaystyle = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } c ( k ) \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( N - k - 1 ) \chi ( \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( N - k - 1 ) \| _ { 2 } > C ) \| _ { 2 } } \end{array}
$$

Hence,

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathbf { E } [ e ^ { \frac { \lambda } { N } \operatorname* { s u p } _ { i \in \mathbf { I } , t \in \mathbb { Z } } \sum _ { t = 1 } ^ { N } \| \vert \mathbf { z } _ { i , t } ( t ) - \bar { \mathbf { z } } _ { i , t } ( t ) \vert \vert _ { 2 } } ] \leq } \\ & { \leq \mathbf { E } [ e ^ { \frac { \lambda } { N } \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } c ( k ) \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( N - k - 1 ) \chi ( \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( N - k - 1 ) \| _ { 2 } > C ) \| _ { 2 } } ] } \\ & { = \underset { L \to \infty } { \operatorname* { l i m } } \underset { k = 0 } { \overset { L } { \prod } } \bar { \Psi } _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } \left( \frac { c ( k ) \lambda } { N } , \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , C \right) } \end{array}
$$

where we applied Lemma C.15 to ${ \bf E } \big [ e ^ { \frac { \lambda } { N } c ( k ) \| e _ { g } ( N - k - 1 ) \chi ( \| { \bf e } _ { g } ( N - k - 1 ) \| _ { 2 } > C ) \| _ { 2 } } \big ]$ with $\bar { \lambda } = \| \bar { \boldsymbol { \beta } } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }$ and $\begin{array} { r } { s = \frac { c ( k ) \lambda } { N } < 0 . 5 \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } } \end{array}$ since $\lambda < 0 . 5 N$ by assumption.

Note that

$$
\begin{array} { l } { \displaystyle { d _ { L } = \ln \left( \prod _ { k = 0 } ^ { L } \bar { \Psi } _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } \left( \frac { c ( k ) \lambda } { N } , \| \bar { \boldsymbol { \beta } } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , C \right) \right) = \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { L } r _ { k } } } \\ { \displaystyle { r _ { k } : = \ln \left( 1 + \frac { c ( k ) \lambda } { N } \frac { \bar { \Psi } _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( \| \bar { \boldsymbol { \beta } } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ) \| \bar { \boldsymbol { \beta } } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } C } { e ^ { \| \bar { \boldsymbol { \beta } } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } C } - 1 } + \left( \frac { c ( k ) \lambda } { N } \right) ^ { 2 } \frac { 2 \bar { \Psi } _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( \| \bar { \boldsymbol { \beta } } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ) } { \| \bar { \boldsymbol { \beta } } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ^ { 2 } } \right) \ge 0 } } \end{array}
$$

We show that the series $\scriptstyle \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } r _ { k }$ is bounded, more precisely,

$$
\sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { L } r _ { k } \leq \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \lambda \frac { \bar { \Psi } _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ) \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } C } { e ^ { \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } C } - 1 } + \frac { \lambda ^ { 2 } } { N } ( \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ) ^ { 2 } \frac { 2 \bar { \Psi } _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ) } { \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ^ { 2 } } = \mathcal { C } ( \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \lambda , N , C )
$$

From this it follows that $d _ { L }$ is convergent, and from

$$
\prod _ { k = 0 } ^ { L } \bar { \Psi } _ { { \bf e } _ { g } } \left( \frac { c ( k ) \lambda } { N } , \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , C \right) = e ^ { d _ { L } }
$$

it follows that

$$
\operatorname* { l i m } _ { L  \infty } \prod _ { k = 0 } ^ { L } \bar { \Psi } _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( \frac { c ( k ) \lambda } { N } , \| \bar { \boldsymbol { \beta } } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , C ) \leq e ^ { \mathcal { C } ( \| \bar { \boldsymbol { \beta } } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \lambda , N , C ) }\tag{51}
$$

$\mathrm { i . e . } .$ , the infinite product is well-defined, and the statement of the lemma holds.

In order to show (51), note that using ln $( 1 + x ) \leq x ,$ , it follows that

$$
r _ { k } \leq \frac { c ( k ) \lambda } { N } \frac { \bar { \Psi } _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ) \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } C } { e ^ { \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } C } - 1 } + \left( \frac { c ( k ) \lambda } { N } \right) ^ { 2 } \frac { 2 \bar { \Psi } _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ) } { \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ^ { 2 } }
$$

Hence,

$$
\sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { L } r _ { k } \leq \frac { \lambda \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { L } c ( k ) } { N } \frac { \bar { \Psi } _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ) \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } C } { e ^ { \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } C } - 1 } + \left( \frac { \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { L } ( c ( k ) ) ^ { 2 } } { N ^ { 2 } } \right) \frac { 2 \bar { \Psi } _ { \mathbf { e } _ { g } } ( \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ) } { \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ^ { 2 } }
$$

From (49) it follows that $c ( k ) \leq \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }$ . Hence

$$
\left( \frac { 1 } { N } c ( k ) \right) ^ { 2 } \leq \frac { 1 } { N ^ { 2 } } c ( k ) \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }
$$

and as from (50) it follows that $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { L } c ( k ) \leq N \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } } \end{array}$ , we get that

$$
\sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { L } \left( \frac { 1 } { N } c ( k ) \right) ^ { 2 } \leq \frac { 1 } { N ^ { 2 } } \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { L } c ( k ) \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \leq \frac { 1 } { N } \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ^ { 2 }
$$

This completes the proof.

Lemma C.17 provides the basis for several technical results.

ProofofLemma C.10. From Lemma B.3 it follows that $\mathbf { y } ( t ) = ( \alpha _ { g , y } \star \mathbf { e } _ { g } ) ( t )$ and $\bar { \mathbf { y } } ( t ) = ( \alpha _ { g , y } \star$ $\bar { \mathbf { e } } _ { g } ) ( t )$ . By setting $I = \{ g \} , \beta _ { i , t } = \alpha _ { g , y } , i = g$ and using Lemma $C . { \overset { \sim } { 1 7 } } .$ , the first inequality follows. Similar, from Lemma B.3 it follows that ${ \hat { \mathbf { y } } } ( t ) = ( c _ { \theta } \star \mathbf { e } _ { g } ) ( t )$ and $\hat { \bar { \mathbf { y } } } ( t ) = ( c _ { \theta } \star \bar { \mathbf { e } } _ { g } ) ( t )$ . By setting $I = \Theta , \beta _ { i , t } = c _ { \theta } , i = \theta \in \Theta$ , it follows that

$$
\mathbf { E } \big [ e ^ { \frac { \lambda } { N } \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \theta \in \Theta } \sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N } \| \hat { \mathbf { y } } ( t ) - \hat { \mathbf { y } } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } } \big ] \leq e ^ { \mathcal { C } ( \| \bar { c } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \lambda , N , C ) }
$$

where $\begin{array} { r } { \bar { c } ( k ) = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \theta \in \Theta } \| c _ { \theta } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } } \end{array}$ . Note that by definition $\begin{array} { r } { c _ { \theta } ( k ) = \sum _ { l = 0 } ^ { k } \alpha _ { \theta } ( l ) \alpha _ { g , w } ( k - l ) } \end{array}$ and hence

$$
\| c _ { \theta } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } \leq \sum _ { l = 0 } ^ { k } \| \alpha _ { \theta } ( l ) \| _ { 2 } \| \alpha _ { g , w } ( k - l ) \| _ { 2 } \leq \sum _ { l = 0 } ^ { k } \| \bar { \alpha } ( l ) \| _ { 2 } \| \alpha _ { g , w } ( k - l ) \| _ { 2 }
$$

In particular,

$$
\bar { c } ( k ) \leq \sum _ { l = 0 } ^ { k } \| \bar { \alpha } ( l ) \| _ { 2 } \| \alpha _ { g , w } ( k - l ) \| _ { 2 }
$$

from which by the well-known property of Cauchy products of series (the infinite sum of the Cauchy product of two absolutely convergent series is equal to the product of their infinite sums) it follows that

$$
\| \bar { c } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \leq \| \bar { \alpha } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \| \alpha _ { g , w } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } .
$$

By noticing that ${ \mathcal { C } } ( \beta , \lambda , N , C )$ is increasing in $\beta ,$ , it follows that $\begin{array} { r l } { \mathcal { C } ( \| \bar { c } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \lambda , N , C ) } & { { } \le } \end{array}$ $\mathcal { C } ( \| \bar { \alpha } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \| \alpha _ { g , w } ^ { - } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \lambda , N , C )$ , from which the second inequality follows.

Finally, the proof of the third inequality is similar to the second one. By Lemma B.3 it follows that $\hat { \mathbf { y } } ( t \mid \hat { 0 } ) = ( \bar { c } _ { \theta , t } { \star } \mathbf { e } _ { g } ) ( t )$ and $\hat { \bar { \mathbf { y } } } ( t | \mathbf { \bar { \mathbf { 0 } } } ) = \dot { ( } c _ { \theta , t } { \star } \bar { \mathbf { e } } _ { g } ) ( t )$ . By applying lemma C.17 to $I = \Theta , \beta _ { i , t } = c _ { \theta , t }$

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathbf { E } \big [ e ^ { \frac { \lambda } { N } \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \theta \in \Theta } \sum _ { t = 0 } ^ { N } \| \hat { \mathbf { y } } ( t | 0 ) - \hat { \mathbf { y } } ( t | 0 ) \| _ { 2 } } \big ] \leq e ^ { \mathcal { C } ( \| \bar { c } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \lambda , N , C ) } } \end{array}
$$

where $\begin{array} { r } { \bar { \bar { c } } ( k ) = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { \theta \in \Theta , t \geq 0 } \| c _ { \theta , t } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } } \end{array}$ . By noticing that

$$
\begin{array} { l } { \displaystyle | | c _ { \theta , t } ( k ) | | _ { 2 } = | | \displaystyle \sum _ { l = 0 } ^ { \operatorname* { m i n } \{ k , t \} } \alpha _ { \theta } ( k ) \alpha _ { g , w } ( k - l ) | | _ { 2 } \le \displaystyle \sum _ { l = 0 } ^ { \operatorname* { m i n } \{ k , t \} } \| \alpha _ { \theta } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } \| \alpha _ { g , w } ( k - l ) \| _ { 2 } } \\ { \displaystyle \le \displaystyle \sum _ { l = 0 } ^ { k } \| \bar { \alpha } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } \| \alpha _ { g , w } ( k - l ) \| _ { 2 } \le \displaystyle \sum _ { l = 0 } ^ { k } \| \bar { \alpha } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } \| \alpha _ { g , w } ( k - l ) \| _ { 2 } , } \end{array}
$$

from which it follows that

$$
\lVert \bar { \bar { c } } \rVert _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \leq \lVert \bar { \alpha } \rVert _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \lVert \alpha _ { g , w } \rVert _ { \ell _ { 1 } }
$$

From the monotonicity of $\mathcal { C } ( s , \lambda , N , C )$ in s, it follows that

$$
\mathcal { C } ( \| \bar { \bar { c } } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \lambda , N , C ) \leq \mathcal { C } ( \| \bar { \alpha } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \| \alpha _ { g , w } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , \lambda , N , C ) ,
$$

which completes the proof.

## C.3 Proof of Theorem 3.1: quadratic loss

The core of the proof is to to show that the prediction errors $\| \mathbf { y } ( t ) - \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \| _ { 2 }$ and $\| \mathbf { y } ( t ) - \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid 0 ) \| .$ 2 are sub-Gaussian and hence they are bounded with high probablity as follows.

Lemma C.18. For $N \geq 1$ and $\delta \in ( 0 , 0 . 5 )$

$$
\mathbf { P } ( \forall \theta \in \Theta , \forall t = 0 , \dots , N - 1 :
$$

$$
\operatorname* { m a x } \{ \| \mathbf { y } ( t ) - \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } , \| \mathbf { y } ( t ) - \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid 0 ) \| _ { 2 } \} \leq 6 G _ { e } ( \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } ) \sqrt { \ln ( \frac { 3 N ^ { 2 } } { 2 \delta } ) } ) \geq 1 - \frac { 2 \delta } { 3 }\tag{56}
$$

and

$$
\mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { y } ( t ) - \mathbf { y } \theta ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } \chi ( \| \mathbf { y } ( t ) - \mathbf { y } \theta ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } > ( 6 G _ { e } \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } ) ^ { 2 } \ln { \Big ( \frac { 3 N ^ { 2 } } { 2 \delta } \Big ) } ) ] \leq \frac { 2 \cdot 6 ^ { 2 } G _ { e } ^ { 2 } \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } ^ { 2 } \ln ( \frac { 3 N ^ { 2 } } { 2 \delta } ) } { N }\tag{57}
$$

We then replace the quadratic loss with a loss which is Lipschitz for the case when $\| y ( t ) - \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t \mid 0 ) \| _ { 2 }$ are bounded. More precisely, let us define the loss function $\ell _ { C }$ as follows:

$$
\ell _ { C } ( y , \hat { y } ) = \operatorname* { m i n } \{ \| y - \hat { y } \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } , C ^ { 2 } \}
$$

Lemma C.19. For any $C > 0 , \ell _ { C }$ is 2C-Lipschitz.

The proof then reduces the quadratic case to the case of Lipschitz loss $\ell _ { C }$ for a suitable C and it goes as follows.

ProofofTheorem 3.1:quadratic case. Let us set $\begin{array} { r } { C = 6 G _ { e } \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } \sqrt { \ln ( \frac { 3 N ^ { 2 } } { 2 \delta } ) } } \end{array}$ and let us consider the true and empirical errores for the loss function $\ell _ { C } \colon$

By applying Theorem 3.1 for the case of Lipschitz loss function $\ell _ { C }$ and with $\frac { 2 \delta } { 3 }$ instead of $\delta ,$ it follows that for all $\epsilon \in \{ - 1 , 1 \}$ , for all $\begin{array} { r } { 0 < \lambda < \frac { N } { 1 6 \cdot 2 C } } \end{array}$

$$

holds with probability at least $1 - \textstyle { \frac { 4 \delta } { 3 } }$ , where for $i = 1 ,$ 2.

$$
\bar { C } _ { i } ( \lambda , \theta , N , \delta , \epsilon ) = \mathcal { C } _ { u b , i } ( \lambda , \theta , 1 2 G _ { e } \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } \sqrt { \ln \left( \frac { 3 N ^ { 2 } } { 2 \delta } \right) } , N )\tag{60}
$$

By using Lemma C.18, (59) and the union bound, it follows that

$$
\begin{array} { l } { \displaystyle \forall \rho \in \mathcal { M } _ { \pi } : \epsilon E _ { \theta \sim \rho } { } ^ { C } \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) \leq \epsilon E _ { \theta \sim \rho } { } ^ { C } \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) + \displaystyle \frac { 1 } { \lambda } \left( \mathrm { K L } ( \rho | | \pi ) + \ln \frac { 3 } { 2 \delta } + \right. } \\ { \displaystyle \left. \frac { 1 } { 2 } \left( \ln E _ { \theta \sim \pi } \exp ( \bar { \mathcal { C } } _ { 1 } ( 2 \lambda , \theta , N , \frac { 2 \delta } { 3 } , \epsilon ) ) + \ln E _ { \theta \sim \pi } \exp ( \bar { \mathcal { C } } _ { 2 } ( 2 \lambda , \theta , N , \frac { 2 \delta } { 3 } , \epsilon ) ) \right) \right. } \end{array}\tag{61}

and

Notice that if $\omega \in \Omega$ is such that

then $\ell _ { C } ( { \mathbf { y } } ( t ) ( \omega ) , \hat { { \mathbf { y } } } _ { \theta } ( t \mid 0 ) ( \omega ) ) = \| { \mathbf { y } } ( t ) ( \omega ) - \hat { { \mathbf { y } } } _ { \theta } ( t \mid 0 ) ( \omega ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 }$ and hence

$$

For $\epsilon = - 1$ we will show (62) implies

\begin{array} { l } { \displaystyle \forall \rho \in \mathcal { M } _ { \pi } : \displaystyle \epsilon E _ { \theta \sim \rho } \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) \leq \epsilon E _ { \theta \sim \rho } \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) + \frac { 1 } { \lambda } \left( \mathrm { K L } ( \rho \| \pi ) + \ln \frac { 3 } { 2 \delta } + \right. } \\ { \displaystyle \left. \frac { 1 } { 2 } \left( \ln E _ { \theta \sim \pi } \exp ( \bar { \mathcal { C } } _ { 1 } ( 2 \lambda , \theta , N , \frac { 2 \delta } { 3 } , \epsilon ) ) + \ln E _ { \theta \sim \pi } \exp ( \bar { \mathcal { C } } _ { 2 } ( 2 \lambda , \theta , N , \frac { 2 \delta } { 3 } , \epsilon ) ) \right) \right) } \end{array}\tag{63}

We will conclude by presenting the proof of Lemma C.18-C.19. To this end, we need the following technical result.

Lemma C.20. Let I be an index set, $\{ \beta _ { i , t } ( k ) \} _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } , i \in { \bf I } ,$ , be a sequence of matrices such that $\| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } < \infty$ , where $\begin{array} { r } { \bar { \beta } ( k ) = \operatorname* { s u p } _ { i \in \mathbf { I } , t \in \mathbb { Z } } \| \beta _ { i , t } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } } \end{array}$ , and let $\mathbf { z } _ { i } ( t ) = ( \beta _ { i , t } \star \mathbf { e } _ { g } ) ( t )$ . $$

$$

( 3 \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } ) ^ { 2 r } = 2 r ! ( 3 \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } ) ^ { 2 r }\tag{73}

( 3 \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } ) ^ { 2 r } = 0 .

$$
( \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \| \bar { \beta } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } ) ^ { 2 r } 2 r ! ( 3 \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } ) ^ { 2 r } = \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ^ { 2 r } 2 r ! ( 3 \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } ) ^ { 2 r }
$$

i.e., (70) holds. By noticing that

$$
\begin{array} { l } { { \displaystyle { \bf E } [ e ^ { \lambda { \bf z } ^ { 2 } ( t ) } ] = 1 + \sum _ { r = 1 } ^ { \infty } \frac { \lambda ^ { r } { \bf E } [ { \bf z } ^ { 2 r } ( t ) ] } { r ! } \le 1 + \sum _ { r = 1 } ^ { \infty } \frac { \lambda ^ { r } \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ^ { 2 r } ( 3 \| { \bf e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } ) ^ { 2 r } 2 r ! } { r ! } = } \ ~ } \\ { { \displaystyle 1 + 2 \lambda \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ^ { 2 } ( 3 \| { \bf e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } ) ^ { 2 } \sum _ { r = 0 } ^ { \infty } ( \lambda \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ^ { 2 } ( 3 \| { \bf e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } ) ^ { 2 } ) ^ { r } = 1 + \frac { 2 \lambda \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ^ { 2 } ( 3 \| { \bf e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } ) ^ { 2 } } { 1 - \lambda \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ^ { 2 } ( 3 \| { \bf e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } ) ^ { 2 } } = } \ ~ } \\ { { \displaystyle \frac { 1 + \lambda \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ( 3 \| { \bf e } _ { g } ( t ) \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } ) ^ { 2 } } { 1 - \lambda \| \bar { \beta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ^ { 2 } ( 3 \| { \bf e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } ) ^ { 2 } } } } \end{array}
$$

it follows that (71) holds. Finally, for $\begin{array} { r } { \lambda = \frac { 1 } { 4 \| \bar { \boldsymbol { \beta } } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } ^ { 2 } ( 3 \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } ) ^ { 2 } } } \end{array}$ , the bound becomes

from which by the definition of sub-Gaussian norm of ${ \bf z } ( t )$ it follows

$$

\begin{array} { r l } & { \eta _ { \Delta } \frac { \sqrt { \langle \Delta \phi \rangle _ { i + 1 } ^ { 2 } } } { \mathcal { Z } } \eta _ { \Delta } \xi _ { i + 1 , \xi } ^ { 2 } | \phi _ { \xi _ { i + 1 } } ^ { 2 } | _ { \phi _ { \xi _ { i } } } ^ { 2 } , } \\ & { = \frac { \phi _ { \Delta } \mathcal { Z } } { 1 9 9 \mathcal { Z } \xi _ { i + 1 , \xi } \mathcal { Z } ^ { 2 } | \phi _ { \xi _ { i + 1 } } | \mathcal { Z } ^ { 2 } | \phi _ { \xi _ { i } } | } } \\ & { \quad = \frac { \phi _ { \Delta } \mathcal { Z } } { 1 9 9 \mathcal { Z } \xi _ { i + 1 , \xi } \mathcal { Z } ^ { 2 } | \phi _ { \xi _ { i } } | } } \\ & { \quad \quad = \frac { 2 \sqrt { \mathcal { Z } } } { 1 9 9 \mathcal { Z } \xi _ { i } \mathcal { Z } | \phi _ { \xi _ { i } } | \mathcal { Z } ^ { 3 } | \phi _ { \xi _ { i } } | \mathcal { Z } ^ { 4 } } | \phi _ { \xi _ { i } } ^ { 2 } | \sqrt { \langle \Delta \xi \rangle _ { i } ^ { 2 } | \phi _ { \xi _ { i } } | \mathcal { Z } ^ { 2 } | \phi _ { \xi _ { i } } | _ { \xi _ { i } , \xi } ^ { 2 } \mathrm { r a r s } | \langle \Delta \xi _ { i } \rangle | _ { \xi _ { i } } ^ { 3 } \frac { | \langle \Delta \xi | \phi _ { \xi _ { i } } | \mathcal { Z } ^ { 3 } | } { \mathcal { Z } } } } \\ &  \quad \quad \le \frac { 2 \sqrt { \mathcal { Z } } }  1 9 \mathcal { Z } \xi _ { i } \mathcal { Z } | \phi _ { \xi _ { i } } | \mathcal { Z } ^ { 3 } | \phi _ { \xi _ { i } } | \mathcal { Z } ^ { 3 } | \phi _ { \xi _ { i } } | \mathcal { Z } ^   \end{array}
$$

Hence, for i = 1, 2,

$$
\begin{array} { c l c r } { \displaystyle { \mathcal { C } _ { i } ( 2 \lambda , \theta , N , \delta , \epsilon ) \leq \frac { L } { 1 2 8 } \operatorname* { m a x } \{ 0 , \epsilon \} + } } \\ { \displaystyle { C _ { u , 1 } + C _ { u , 2 } + \frac { 1 } { 2 } \mathcal { C } _ { b , m , i } \left( 1 / 4 , \bar { \alpha } , G _ { e } + 2 G _ { e , 1 } , 1 , \frac { 1 } { \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { 2 } K } \right) } } \end{array}\tag{85}
$$

and thus

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \frac { 1 } { 2 } ( \ln E _ { \theta \sim \pi } \exp ( \mathcal { C } _ { 1 } ( \lambda , \theta , N , \delta , \epsilon ) ) + \ln E _ { \theta \sim \pi } \exp ( \mathcal { C } _ { 2 } ( \lambda , \theta , N , \delta , \epsilon ) ) )  } \\ & {  \le \cfrac { L } { 1 2 8 } \operatorname* { m a x } \{ 0 , \epsilon \} + \cfrac { 1 } { 2 } \cfrac { 2 } { i = 1 } ( C _ { u , 1 } + C _ { u , 2 } + \cfrac { 1 } { 2 } \mathcal { C } _ { b , m , i } ( 1 / 4 , \bar { \alpha } , G _ { e } + 2 G _ { e , 1 } , 1 , \frac { 1 } { \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { 2 } K } ) ) } \\ & { = \mathcal { C } ( \delta , \epsilon ) } \end{array}
$$

which completes the proof.

## C.5 Uniform bounds derived from Corollary 3.2

In order to present the uniform bounds, we need the following assumptions from Assumption 3.3: (LipModel) and either (Gauss) or (Uni).

Corollary C.21 (Uniform [PAC bounds, Example 2.1,2.2 [2]). With the Assumption (LipModel) and either (Gauss) or (Uni) from Assumption 3.3 the following holds. Let $L _ { \Theta } ( \delta ) , \bar { \mathcal { C } } _ { L , e m p } , \bar { \mathcal { C } } _ { L , t r u e } , C ( \theta )$ $r _ { L i p , 1 } , r _ { L i p , 2 }$ be defined as in Table 1, and let $L , \dot { C } _ { \delta } , \mathcal { C } ( \delta , \epsilon )$ , r be defined as in Corollary 3.2 For any

δ ∈ (0, 0.5), λ, σ > 0, N ≥ 3, ϵ ∈ {−1, 1},

$$
\begin{array} { r l r } {  { \forall \theta \in \Theta : \epsilon \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) \leq \epsilon \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) + } } \\ & { } & { \frac { L ( \ln ( N ) ) ^ { r + 1 } } { \sqrt { N } } ( \mathcal { C } _ { L , e m p } + \mathcal { C } _ { L , t r u e } + C ( \theta ) + \ln ( \frac { C _ { \delta } } { \delta ^ { 2 r _ { L | p , 1 } + 1 } } ) + \mathcal { C } ( \delta , \epsilon ) ) } \end{array}\tag{86}
$$

holds with probability at least $1 - ( 2 + r _ { L i p , 1 } ) \delta$ over data.

The proof of Corollary C.21 is based on a number of technical lemmas, which will also be used for the proof of Corollary 3.4.

Lemma C.22 (KL divergence). Assume that either (Gauss) or (Uni) holds. For every $\theta \in \Theta ;$ , define the density ρ<sub>θ</sub> as follows

$$
\rho _ { \theta } = \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { \mathcal { N } ( \theta , \frac { \sigma ^ { 2 } } { N } I ) } & { i f ( \pmb { G a u s s } ) h o l d s } \\ { \mathcal { U } ( \theta , \frac { \sigma } { \sqrt { N } } I ) } & { i f ( U n i ) h o l d s } \end{array} \right.
$$

where ${ \mathcal { N } } ( m , \sigma ^ { 2 } I )$ denotes the Gaussian distribution with mean m and variance $\sigma ^ { 2 } I ,$ and $\mathcal { U } ( m , r )$ denotes the uniform distribution on the ball with center in m and radius r. Thenfor $N \geq 3 ,$

$$
\operatorname { K L } ( \rho _ { \theta } \mid \pi ) \leq C ( \theta ) \ln ( N )
$$

ProofofLemma C.22. If (Gauss) holds, then using KL divergence for Gaussian densities ([25, Lemma 11.3.4]) it follows that

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathrm { K L } ( \rho _ { \theta } \mid \pi ) = } \\ & { \quad \frac { 1 } { 2 } \left( \mathrm { t r a c e } \left( \frac { \sigma ^ { 2 } } { N } P ^ { - 1 } \right) + ( \theta _ { m } - \theta ) ^ { T } P ^ { - 1 } ( \theta _ { m } - \theta ) - n _ { \theta } + \ln \frac { \operatorname* { d e t } ( P ) N ^ { n _ { \theta } } } { \sigma ^ { 2 n _ { \theta } } } \right) } \\ & { \quad = \displaystyle \frac { 1 } { 2 } \left( \mathrm { t r a c e } \left( \frac { \sigma ^ { 2 } } { N } P ^ { - 1 } \right) + ( \theta _ { m } - \theta ) ^ { T } P ^ { - 1 } ( \theta _ { m } - \theta ) - n _ { \theta } + \ln \frac { \operatorname* { d e t } ( P ) } { \sigma ^ { 2 n _ { \theta } } } + n _ { \theta } \ln ( N ) \right) } \\ & { \quad \le \ln ( N ) C ( \theta ) } \end{array}
$$

where we used the fact that $\ln ( N ) \geq 1$ and $\begin{array} { r } { \frac { 1 } { N } \leq 1 \mathrm { i f } N \geq 3 } \end{array}$ . Likewise, if (Uni) holds, and we denote by $C _ { 2 }$ the volume of the ball of radius $\frac { \sigma } { \sqrt { N } }$ centered at ${ \tilde { \theta } } ,$ then

$$
\begin{array} { l } { { \displaystyle { \cal K } L ( \rho _ { \theta } \mid \pi ) = \int _ { \| \widetilde { \theta } - \theta \| _ { 2 } \leq \frac { \sigma ^ { 2 } } { \sqrt { N } } } \ln \left( \frac { \rho _ { \theta } ( \widetilde { \theta } ) } { \pi ( \widetilde { \theta } ) } \right) \frac { 1 } { C _ { 2 } } d m ( \widetilde { \theta } ) } } \\ { { \displaystyle = \int _ { \| \widetilde { \theta } - \theta \| _ { 2 } \leq \frac { \sigma ^ { 2 } } { \sqrt { N } } } \frac { 1 } { C _ { 2 } } \ln \left( \frac { \mathrm { v o l } ( \Theta ) } { C _ { 2 } } \right) d m ( \widetilde { \theta } ) = \ln \left( \frac { \mathrm { v o l } ( \Theta ) } { C _ { 2 } } \right) } } \end{array}
$$

From the formula for the volume of a high-dimensional ball [40] it follows that

$$
C _ { 2 } = \frac { \pi ^ { n _ { \theta } / 2 } } { \Gamma ( n _ { \theta } / 2 + 1 ) } \left( \frac { \sigma } { \sqrt { N } } \right) ^ { n _ { \theta } }
$$

and hence

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathrm { K L } ( \rho _ { \theta } \mid \pi ) = \ln \left( \frac { \mathrm { v o l } ( \Theta ) } { C _ { 2 } } \right) = \ln \left( \frac { \mathrm { v o l } ( \Theta ) } { \frac { \pi ^ { n _ { \theta } / 2 } } { \Gamma \left( n _ { \theta } / 2 + 1 \right) } \left( \frac { \sigma } { \sqrt { N } } \right) ^ { n _ { \theta } } } \right) } \\ & { = \ln \left( \frac { \mathrm { v o l } ( \Theta ) \Gamma \left( n _ { \theta } / 2 + 1 \right) } { \pi ^ { n _ { \theta } / 2 } ( \sigma ) ^ { n _ { \theta } } } \right) + \ln \left( \left( \sqrt { N } \right) ^ { n _ { \theta } } \right) } \\ & { = \ln \left( \frac { \mathrm { v o l } ( \Theta ) \Gamma \left( n _ { \theta } / 2 + 1 \right) } { \sqrt { \pi \sigma ^ { 2 } } ^ { n _ { \theta } } } \right) + \frac { n _ { \theta } } { 2 } \ln ( N ) \le C ( \theta ) \ln ( N ) } \end{array}
$$

Another ingredient is the following:

Lemma C.23 (Lipschitz continuity of the true and empirical errores). Using the constants defined in Corollary 3.2-C.21, thefollowing inequality holds

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \forall \theta _ { 1 } , \theta _ { 2 } \in \Theta : } \\ & { | \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta _ { 1 } ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta _ { 2 } ) | \leq L _ { \Theta } ( \delta ) L ( \ln ( N ) ) ^ { r } \| \theta _ { 1 } - \theta _ { 2 } \| _ { 2 } + } \\ & { \qquad 2 r _ { L i p , I } \frac { L \ln ( N ) ^ { r } } { \sqrt { N } } \left( C _ { u , 1 } + C _ { u , 2 } + \ln \left( \frac { 1 } { \delta } \right) \right) , } \end{array}\tag{87}

with probability $1 - r _ { L i p , 1 } \delta$ over data, where

$$
r _ { L i p , 1 } = \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { 0 } & { { \mathbf { e } } _ { g } i s b o u n d e d } \\ { 1 } & { o t h e r w i s e } \end{array} \right.

and

\begin{array} { l } { \displaystyle \forall \theta _ { 1 } , \theta _ { 2 } \in \Theta : } \\ { \displaystyle | { \mathcal L } ( \theta _ { 1 } ) - { \mathcal L } ( \theta _ { 2 } ) | \leq L _ { \Theta } ( \delta ) L ( \ln ( N ) ) ^ { r } \| \theta _ { 1 } - \theta _ { 2 } \| _ { 2 } + 2 r _ { L i p , 1 } \frac { L \ln ( N ) ^ { r } } { \sqrt { N } } \left( C _ { u , 1 } + C _ { u , 2 } \right) + } \\ { \displaystyle \qquad 2 r _ { L i p , 2 } \frac { L } { 5 1 2 } \frac { L ( \ln ( N ) ) ^ { r } } { \sqrt { N } } } \end{array}\tag{88}
$$

where

$$
r _ { L i p , 2 } = \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { { 2 } } & { { \ell ~ i s ~ q u a d r a t i c ~ a n d ~ { \bf e } _ { g } ~ i s ~ u n b o u n d e d } } \\ { { 0 } } & { { o t h e r w i s e } } \end{array} \right.
$$

Moreover, the inequalities below

(10)from Corollary 3.2 holds, and

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \forall \theta _ { 1 } , \theta _ { 2 } \in \Theta : } \\ & { | \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta _ { 1 } ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta _ { 2 } ) | \leq L _ { \Theta } ( \delta ) L ( \ln ( N ) ) ^ { r } \| \theta _ { 1 } - \theta _ { 2 } \| _ { 2 } + } \\ & { \qquad 2 r _ { L i p , 1 } \frac { L \ln ( N ) ^ { r } } { \sqrt { N } } \left( C _ { u , 1 } + C _ { u , 2 } + \ln \left( \frac { 1 } { \delta } \right) \right) , } \end{array}
$$

and

$$
\begin{array} { l } { \displaystyle { \vert { \mathcal L } ( \theta _ { 1 } ) - { \mathcal L } ( \theta _ { 2 } ) \vert \leq L _ { \Theta } ( \delta ) L ( \ln ( N ) ) ^ { r } \vert \vert \theta _ { 1 } - \theta _ { 2 } \vert \vert _ { 2 } + 2 r _ { L i p , 1 } \frac { L \ln ( N ) ^ { r } } { \sqrt { N } } \left( C _ { u , 1 } + C _ { u , 2 } \right) + } } \\ { \displaystyle { 2 r _ { L i p , 2 } \frac { L } { 5 1 2 } \frac { L ( \ln ( N ) ) ^ { r } } { \sqrt { N } } } } \end{array}\tag{89}

hold simultaneously with probability at least $\begin{array} { r } { 1 - ( 2 + r _ { L i p , 1 } ) \delta . } \end{array}$

Proof of Lemma C.23. Bounded noise case: If ℓ is $L _ { \ell ^ { - } } \mathrm { L }$ ipschitz and $\mathbf { e } _ { g }$ is bounded, then using (LipModel) and the argument of the proof from Lemma B.3, it follows that

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { | \ell ( { \mathbf { y } } ( t ) , { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta _ { 1 } } ( t ) ) - \ell ( { \mathbf { y } } ( t ) , { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta _ { 2 } } ( t ) ) | \leq L _ { \ell } \| { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta _ { 1 } } ( t ) - { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta _ { 2 } } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } } \\ & { \leq L _ { \ell } \displaystyle \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \| c _ { \theta _ { 1 } } ( k ) - c _ { \theta _ { 2 } } ( k ) \| _ { 2 } \| { \mathbf { e } } _ { g } ( t - k ) \| _ { 2 } \leq L _ { \ell } \displaystyle \sum _ { k = 0 } ^ { \infty } \sum _ { l = 0 } ^ { k } \| \alpha _ { \theta _ { 1 } } ( l ) - \alpha _ { \theta _ { 2 } } ( l ) \| _ { 2 } \| \alpha _ { g , w } ( k - l ) \| _ { 2 } c _ { \mathbf { e } } \sqrt { n _ { \mathbf { e } } } } \\ & { \leq L _ { \ell } \| \alpha _ { \theta _ { 1 } } - \alpha _ { \theta _ { 2 } } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } c _ { \mathbf { e } } \sqrt { n _ { \mathbf { e } } } = \underbrace { L _ { \alpha } L _ { \ell } \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } c _ { \mathbf { e } } \sqrt { n _ { \mathbf { e } } } } _ { L _ { \phi } ( \delta ) } \| \theta _ { 1 } - \theta _ { 2 } \| _ { 2 } } \end{array}
$$

From this it then follows that

$$
| \mathcal { L } ( \theta _ { 1 } ) - \mathcal { L } ( \theta _ { 2 } ) | \leq L _ { \Theta } ( \delta ) \| \theta _ { 1 } - \theta _ { 2 } \| \quad \forall \theta _ { 1 } , \theta _ { 2 } \in \Theta\tag{90}
$$

$$
| \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta _ { 1 } ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta _ { 2 } ) | \leq L _ { \Theta } ( \delta ) \| \theta _ { 1 } - \theta _ { 2 } \| \quad \forall \theta _ { 1 } , \theta _ { 2 } \in \Theta\tag{91}
$$

By noticing that $r = 0$ and $L = 1$ for the bounded case, the claim of the lemma follows.

Unbounded noise case: Assume that the noise is not necessarily bounded and ℓ is L<sub>ℓ</sub>-Lipschitz. From Subsection C.2, recall the definition of truncated empirical and true errors $\bar { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta )$ and $\bar { \mathcal { L } } ( \boldsymbol { \theta } )$ corresponding to the output $\bar { \bf y } ( t )$ and $\bar { \bf y } _ { \theta } ( t )$ and $\bar { \bf y } _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ( t \mid 0 )$ generated for the truncated noise $\bar { \mathbf { e } } _ { g } ( t ) =$ $\mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) \chi ( \mathbf { e } _ { g } ( t ) < C )$ for $\begin{array} { r } { C = \frac { \ln ( N ) } { K \lVert \alpha _ { g } \rVert _ { \ell _ { 1 } } } } \end{array}$ . Using Chernoff’s inequality and Lemma C.11, it follows that

$$
\mathbf { P } ( \forall \theta \in \Theta : \left| \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) - \bar { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) \right| > c ) \leq \frac { e ^ { \lambda \frac { 1 } { 2 } \left( \mathcal { C } ( \| \bar { \alpha } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , 2 L _ { \ell } \lambda , N ) + \mathcal { C } ( \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , 2 L _ { \ell } \lambda , N ) \right) } } { e ^ { \lambda c } }\tag{92}
$$

and hence

$$
\mathbf { P } ( \forall \theta \in \Theta : | { \hat { \mathcal { L } } } _ { N } ( \theta ) - { \bar { \mathcal { L } } } _ { N } ( \theta ) | \leq { \frac { 1 } { 2 \lambda } } ( { \mathcal { C } } ( \| { \bar { \alpha } } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , 2 L _ { \ell } \lambda , N ) + 
$$

$$
\mathcal { C } ( \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , 2 L _ { \ell } \lambda , N ) + \ln ( \frac { 1 } { \delta } ) ) \geq 1 - \delta\tag{93}
$$

Similarly, from Lemma C.11 it follows that

$$
\forall \theta \in \Theta : \quad | \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) - \bar { \mathcal { L } } ( \theta ) | \leq \frac { 1 } { 2 \lambda } \left( \mathcal { C } ( \| \bar { \alpha } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , 2 L _ { \ell } \lambda , N ) + \mathcal { C } ( \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , 2 L _ { \ell } \lambda , N ) \right)\tag{94}
$$

From (43) in Subsection C.2 for $\begin{array} { r } { C = \frac { \ln ( N ) } { K \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } } } \end{array}$ it follows that

$$
\frac { 1 } { 2 } \left( \mathcal { C } ( \| \bar { \alpha } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , 2 L _ { \ell } \lambda , N ) + \mathcal { C } ( \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , 2 L _ { \ell } \lambda , N ) \right) = 2 \bar { \mathcal { C } } _ { u b } ( \frac { \lambda } { 8 } , L _ { \ell } , N )
$$

and hence by setting $\begin{array} { r } { \lambda = \frac { \sqrt { N } } { 1 6 L _ { \ell } \ln ( N ) } } \end{array}$ it follows that

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \displaystyle \frac { 1 } { \lambda } ( \frac { 1 } { 2 } ( { \mathcal { C } } ( \| { \bar { \alpha } } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , 2 L _ { \ell } \lambda , N ) + { \mathcal { C } } ( \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } , 2 L _ { \ell } \lambda , N ) ) } \\ & { \displaystyle \leq ( \frac { 1 } { 2 } C _ { u , 1 } + \frac { 1 } { 8 } C _ { u , 2 } ) \frac { 1 6 L _ { \ell } \ln ( N ) } { \sqrt { N } } \leq ( C _ { u , 1 } + C _ { u , 2 } ) \frac { 1 6 L _ { \ell } \ln ( N ) } { \sqrt { N } } } \end{array}\tag{95}
$$

and hence, appplying it to (93)-(94) and recalling that $L = 1 6 L _ { \ell }$ results in

\mathbf { P } \left( \forall \theta \in \Theta : \left| \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) - \bar { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) \right| \leq ( C _ { u , 1 } + C _ { u , 2 } + \mathrm { l n } ( \frac { 1 } { \delta } ) ) \frac { 1 6 L _ { \ell } \ln ( N ) } { \sqrt { N } } \right) \geq 1 - \delta\tag{96}

\forall \theta _ { 1 } , \theta _ { 2 } \in \Theta : | \mathcal { L } ( \theta _ { 1 } ) - \mathcal { L } ( \theta _ { 2 } ) | \leq ( C _ { u , 1 } + C _ { u , 2 } ) \frac { L \ln ( N ) } { \sqrt { N } }

Note that (90)-(91) holds for $\bar { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \boldsymbol { \theta } )$ and $\bar { \hat { \mathcal { L } } } _ { N } ( \theta )$ with $L _ { \Theta } ( \delta ) = L _ { \alpha } L _ { \ell } \sqrt { n _ { \mathbf { e } } } \frac { \ln ( N ) } { K \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } }$ and hence by using (96) and the observation

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { | \hat { \mathcal L } _ { N } ( \theta _ { 1 } ) - \hat { \mathcal L } _ { N } ( \theta _ { 2 } ) | \leq | \hat { \mathcal L } _ { N } ( \theta _ { 1 } ) - \bar { \hat { \mathcal L } } _ { N } ( \theta _ { 1 } ) | + | \bar { \hat { \mathcal L } } _ { N } ( \theta _ { 1 } ) - \bar { \hat { \mathcal L } } _ { N } ( \theta _ { 2 } ) | + | \bar { \hat { \mathcal L } } _ { N } ( \theta _ { 2 } ) - \hat { \mathcal L } _ { N } ( \theta _ { 2 } ) | } \\ & { | { \mathcal L } ( \theta _ { 1 } ) - { \mathcal L } ( \theta _ { 2 } ) | \leq | { \mathcal L } ( \theta _ { 1 } ) - \bar { \mathcal L } ( \theta _ { 1 } ) | + | \bar { \mathcal L } ( \theta _ { 1 } ) - \bar { \mathcal L } ( \theta _ { 2 } ) | + | \bar { \mathcal L } ( \theta _ { 2 } ) - { \mathcal L } ( \theta _ { 2 } ) | } \end{array}

it follows

$$
\mathbf { P } \left( \forall \theta _ { 1 } , \theta _ { 2 } \in \Theta : \left| \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta _ { 1 } ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta _ { 1 } ) \right| \leq 2 ( C _ { u , 1 } + C _ { u , 2 } + \mathrm { l n } ( \frac { 1 } { \delta } ) ) \frac { 1 6 L _ { \ell } \ln ( N ) } { \sqrt { N } } ) + \right.
$$

$$
\begin{array} { l } { { \displaystyle L _ { \alpha } L _ { \ell } \sqrt { n _ { \mathbf { e } } } \frac { \ln ( N ) } { K \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } } \| \theta _ { 1 } - \theta _ { 2 } \| _ { 2 } \bigg ) \geq 1 - \delta } } \\ { { \displaystyle \forall \theta _ { 1 } , \theta _ { 2 } \in \Theta : | \mathcal { L } ( \theta _ { 1 } ) - \mathcal { L } ( \theta _ { 2 } ) | } } \\  { \displaystyle \leq 2 ( C _ { u , 1 } + C _ { u , 2 } + \ln ( \frac { 1 } { \delta } ) ) \frac { L \ln ( N ) } { \sqrt { N } } ) + L _ { \alpha } L _ { \ell } \sqrt { n _ { \mathbf { e } } } \frac { \ln ( N ) } { K \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } } \| \theta _ { 1 } - \theta _ { 2 } \| _ { 2 } } \end{array}\tag{97}
$$

Since $L _ { \alpha } L _ { \ell } \sqrt { n _ { \mathbf { e } } } \frac { \ln ( N ) } { K \| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } } } = L _ { \Theta } L \ln ( N )$ with the definition of $L _ { \Theta }$ as in Corollary C.21, (97) can be rewritten as

$$
\mathbf { P } \big ( \forall \theta _ { 1 } , \theta _ { 2 } \in \Theta : \Big | \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta _ { 1 } ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta _ { 1 } ) \Big | \leq 2 \big ( C _ { u , 1 } + C _ { u , 2 } + \mathrm { l n } ( \frac { 1 } { \delta } ) \big ) \frac { L \mathrm { l n } ( N ) } { \sqrt { N } } \big ) +

## D Proof of Corollary 3.4

We start with the following remark explaining the notion of random models drawn from a datadependent posterior.

Remark D.1 (Joint probability over data and parameters). Let $\rho ^ { \mathcal { D } }$ be afunction on $\Omega \times \Theta$ which is measurable w.r.t. the direct product $\mathcal { F } \times B _ { \Theta } o f \sigma \cdot a l g e b r a s ,$ , and such thatfor any $\omega \in \Omega , \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } ( \omega )$ $\Theta \ni \Theta \mapsto \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } ( \omega , \theta )$ is a probability density on Θ. We refer to $\rho ^ { \mathcal { D } }$ as a random density on Ω. Let B be a subset $\boldsymbol { \mathcal { I } } \boldsymbol { \mathcal { F } } \boldsymbol { \times } \boldsymbol { B } _ { \Theta }$ and denote by $P _ { \theta \sim \rho ^ { D } } ( B )$ the random variable ω 7→ $P _ { \theta \sim \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } ( \omega ) } ( \{ \theta \mid ( \omega , \theta ) \in B \} )$ ), where $P _ { \theta \sim \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } ( \omega ) }$ is the probability measure induced by the density $\rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } ( \omega )$ . Define the probability measure $\mathbf { P } \times P _ { \theta \sim \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } }$ on $\mathcal { F } \times B _ { \Theta }$ asfollows:

$$
\left( \mathbf { P } \times P _ { \theta \sim \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } } \right) ( B ) \triangleq \mathbf { E } [ P _ { \theta \sim \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } } ( B ) ]
$$

Similarly to the previous definition ofrelationship holding with probability over data, $i f \diamond$ is a binary operator and $\{ X _ { i } , Y _ { i } \} _ { i \in I }$ is family of functions taking values in R and which are measurable w.r.t. $( \bar { \boldsymbol { \Omega } } \times \boldsymbol { \Theta } , \mathcal { F } \times \dot { \boldsymbol { B } } _ { \Theta } )$ , then we say that $\begin{array} { r } { \dot { \forall } i \in I : X _ { i } \diamond \bar { Y _ { i } } } \end{array}$ , holds with probability at least $1 - \delta$ over data and over samplesfrom $\rho ^ { \tilde { \mathcal { D } } }$ , if the set $B = \{ ( \omega , \theta ) \in \Omega \times \Theta \mid \forall i \in I : X _ { i } ( \omega , \theta ) \circ Y _ { i } ( \omega , \theta ) \}$ is measurable in $( \Omega \times \Theta , { \mathcal F } \times B _ { \Theta } )$ and $\left( \mathbf { P } \times P _ { \theta \sim \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } } \right) ( B ) \geq 1 - \delta$

Note that if a relationship holds with a probability at least $1 - \delta$ over data, then it also holds with a probability at least $1 - \delta$ over data and sampled from $\rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } \colon i f F$ is an event from ${ \mathcal F } ,$ then $\left( \mathbf { P } \times { \hat { P } } _ { \theta \sim \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } } \right) ( { \dot { F } } ) = \mathbf { P } ( F ) \geq 1 - \delta .$

Lemma D.2 (Property of Gibbs distribution).

$$
