In particular, ifthe Gibbs posterior $\rho ^ { G i b b s }$ is definedfor $\begin{array} { r } { \lambda = \lambda _ { N } = \frac { \sqrt { N } } { L ( \ln ( N ) ) ^ { r } } } \end{array}$ then for $N \geq K _ { \delta }$

$$
\mathcal { L } ( \theta _ { \star } ) \leq - \frac { 1 } { \lambda } \ln E _ { \theta \sim \pi } e ^ { - \lambda \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) } + \frac { L ( \ln ( N ) ) ^ { r } } { \sqrt { N } } \left( \ln \frac { C _ { \delta } } { \delta } + \mathcal { C } ( \delta , 1 ) \right)\tag{119}
$$

ProofofLemma D.3. Following the argument of the proof of [2, Theorem 2.7], it follows that for any measurable function X on $\begin{array} { r } { \Omega \times \Theta , E _ { \theta \sim \pi } e ^ { 2 \lambda X ( \omega , \theta ) } = E _ { \theta \sim \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } } e ^ { 2 \lambda X ( \omega , \theta ) - \ln \frac { \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } ( \omega , \theta ) } { \pi ( \theta ) } } } \end{array}$ and hence $\begin{array} { r } { \mathbf { E } E _ { \theta \sim \pi } e ^ { 2 \lambda X ( \theta ) } = \mathbf { E } E _ { \theta \sim \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } } e ^ { 2 \lambda X ( \theta ) - \ln \frac { \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } ( \theta ) } { \pi ( \theta ) } } } \end{array}$ with the random variables $X ( \theta ) : \omega \ni \Omega \mapsto X ( \omega , \theta )$ Notice that $\mathbf { E } E _ { \theta \sim \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } }$ is the expectation operator corresponding to the probability measure $\mathbf { P } \times P _ { \theta \sim \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } }$ and that $\mathbf { E } [ E _ { \theta \sim \pi } { \bar { X } } ] = E _ { \theta \sim \pi } \mathbf { \dot { E } } [ X ]$ , and hence, by Chernoff’s bound:

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \mathbf { P } \times P _ { \theta \sim \rho ^ { D } } \big ( X < \frac { 1 } { 2 \lambda } \big ( \ln \frac { 1 } { \delta } + \ln \frac { \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } ( \theta ) } { \pi ( \theta ) } + \ln \mathbf { E } E _ { \theta \sim \pi } e ^ { 2 \lambda X } \big ) \big ) } \\ & { = \mathbf { P } \times P _ { \theta \sim \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } } \big ( ( X - \frac { 1 } { 2 \lambda } \ln \frac { \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } ( \theta ) } { \pi ( \theta ) } ) < \frac { 1 } { 2 \lambda } \big ( \ln \frac { 1 } { \delta } + \ln \mathbf { E } E _ { \theta \sim \pi } e ^ { 2 \lambda X } \big ) \big ) \le \frac { \mathbf { E } E { E } _ { \theta \sim \rho ^ { D } } e ^ { 2 \lambda X - \ln \frac { \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } ( \theta ) } { \pi ( \theta ) } } } { e ^ { \ln \frac { 1 } { \delta } + \ln \mathbf { E } E _ { \theta \sim \pi } e ^ { 2 \lambda X } } } } \\ & { = \frac { \mathbf { E } E _ { \theta \sim \pi } e ^ { 2 \lambda X } } { \frac { 1 } { \delta } \mathbf { E } E _ { \theta \sim \pi } e ^ { 2 \lambda X } } = \delta } \end{array}
$$

Hence,

$$
X \leq \frac { 1 } { 2 \lambda } \big ( \ln \frac { 1 } { \delta } + \ln \frac { \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } ( \theta ) } { \pi ( \theta ) } + \ln E _ { \theta \sim \pi } \mathbf { E } e ^ { 2 \lambda X ( \theta ) } \big )\tag{120}
$$

with probability at least $1 - \delta$ over data and samples from $\rho ^ { \mathcal { D } }$ . Hence, by taking $X = \epsilon ( \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) - V _ { N } ( \theta ) )$ and then $X = \epsilon ( V _ { N } ( \theta ) ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) )$ , and by taking the union bound as in the proof of Theorem C.1, it follows that

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \displaystyle \mathcal { L } ( \boldsymbol { \theta } ) \leq \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \boldsymbol { \theta } ) + \frac { 1 } { \lambda } \Big ( \ln \frac { 1 } { \delta } + \ln \frac { \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } ( \boldsymbol { \theta } ) } { \pi ( \boldsymbol { \theta } ) } } \\ & { \quad \quad \quad + \frac { 1 } { 2 } \Big ( \ln E _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } \sim \pi } \mathbf { E } [ e ^ { 2 \epsilon \lambda ( V _ { N } ( \boldsymbol { \theta } ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \boldsymbol { \theta } ) ) } ] + \ln E _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } \sim \pi } \mathbf { E } [ e ^ { 2 \epsilon \lambda ( \mathcal { L } ( \boldsymbol { \theta } ) - V _ { N } ( \boldsymbol { \theta } ) ) } ] \Big ) \Big ) } \end{array}\tag{121}
$$

Proof of (116) for bounded noise Let us apply the inequality (121) and use the bound on $\mathbf { E } [ e ^ { 2 \epsilon \lambda ( V _ { N } ( \theta ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) ) } ]$ and $\mathbf { E } [ e ^ { 2 \epsilon \lambda ( \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) - V _ { N } ( \theta ) ) } ]$ from Lemma C.6-C.5, from which (116) follows.

Proof of (116) for Lipschitz loss Let us apply (44) from Subsection C.2 to bound the bound on $\mathbf { E } [ e ^ { 2 \epsilon \lambda ( V _ { N } ( \theta ) - \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) ) } ]$ and $\mathbf { E } [ e ^ { 2 \epsilon \lambda ( \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) - V _ { N } ( \theta ) ) } ]$ , from which (116) follows.

Proof of (116) quadratic loss Recall from Subsection C.3 the definition of the losses $^ C \mathcal L ( \theta ) , ^ { C } \hat { \mathcal L } _ { N } ( \theta )$ corresponding to the loss $\ell _ { C }$ . Note that by Lemma ${ \bf C . l 9 } \ell _ { C }$ is 2C-Lipschitz. Notice that from the statement of the present lemma proven for the case of Lipschitz losses for $\begin{array} { r } { \lambda \le \frac { \sqrt { N } } { 1 6 \cdot 2 C \ln ( N ) } } \end{array}$ , with $C = 6 G _ { e } \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } \sqrt { \mathrm { l n } ( \frac { 3 N ^ { 2 } } { 2 \delta } ) }$ probability $1 - { \textstyle \frac { 4 } { 3 \delta } }$ over data and parameters sampled from $\rho ^ { \mathcal { D } }$ , the following holds:

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \epsilon ^ { C } \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) \leq \epsilon ^ { C } \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) + \displaystyle \frac { 1 } { \lambda } \left( \ln \left( \frac { \rho ( \theta ) } { \pi ( \theta ) } \right) + \ln \frac { 3 } { 2 \delta } + \right. } \\ & { \left. \frac { 1 } { 2 } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 } \ln \mathbf { E } _ { \theta \sim \pi } \exp \left( \bar { C } _ { i } ( 2 \lambda , \theta , N , \frac { 2 \delta } { 3 } , \epsilon ) \right) \right) } \end{array}\tag{122}
$$

where $\begin{array} { r } { \bar { C } _ { i } ( 2 \lambda , \theta , N , \frac { 2 \delta } { 3 } , \epsilon ) } \end{array}$ satisfies (60). From Lemma C.18 it follows that (56) holds with probability at least $\begin{array} { r } { 1 - \frac { 2 \delta } { 3 } } \end{array}$ over data and hence (C.18) holds with probability at least $\begin{array} { r } { 1 - \frac { 2 \delta } { 3 } } \end{array}$ over data and parameters sampled from $\rho ^ { \theta }$ . Hence, by using the union bound, it follows that (122) and (56) hold simultaneously with probability at least 1 − 2δ over data and parameters sampled from $\rho ^ { \mathcal { D } }$

Moreover, if (57) holds, then it holds that $^ C \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) = \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta )$ and by and (68) – (67)

$$
^ C \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) \leq \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) \leq ^ { C } \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) + 7 2 G _ { e } ^ { 2 } \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } ^ { 2 } \frac { \ln \left( \frac { 3 N ^ { 2 } } { 2 \delta } \right) } { N }
$$

$$
\epsilon \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) \leq \epsilon ^ { C } \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) + 7 2 G _ { e } ^ { 2 } \| \mathbf { e } _ { g } \| _ { \psi _ { 2 } } ^ { 2 } \frac { \ln \left( \frac { 3 N ^ { 2 } } { 2 \delta } \right) } { N } \operatorname* { m a x } \{ \epsilon , 0 \}\tag{123}
$$

Hence, by using (122) and $^ C \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) = \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta )$ , it follows that if (123) and (56) both hold, then (116) holds. Since (123) and (56) holds with probability at least 1 − 2δ over data and samples from $\rho ^ { \mathcal { D } }$ , so does (116).

Proof of (116) implies (118) It follows by applying Lemma D.2.

Proof of (117) From the proof of Corollary 3.2 it follows that for $\begin{array} { r } { \lambda = \frac { \sqrt { N } } { L ( \ln ( N ) ) ^ { r } } } \end{array}$ where r is as in Corollary 3.2,

$$
\frac { 1 } { 2 \lambda } \left( \ln E _ { \theta \sim \pi } \exp ( \mathcal { C } _ { 1 } ( 2 \lambda , \theta , N , \delta , \epsilon ) ) + \ln E _ { \theta \sim \pi } \exp ( \mathcal { C } _ { 2 } ( 2 \lambda , \theta , N , \delta , \epsilon ) ) \right) \le \mathcal { C } ( \delta , \epsilon )

Using this inequality, it follows that (116) implies (117).

Lemma D.4. $I f \mathcal { L } ( \theta )$ is convex, $\rho$ is a probability density over $\Theta ,$ , and $\theta _ { \star } = E _ { \theta \sim \rho } \theta$ is the mean of the density $\rho ,$ then

If $\mathcal { L } ( \boldsymbol { \theta } )$ is convex in $\theta ,$ then $\mathcal { L } ( \theta _ { \star } ) = \mathcal { L } ( E _ { \theta \sim \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } } \theta ) \leq E _ { \theta \sim \rho ^ { \mathcal { D } } } \mathcal { L } ( \theta )$ by Jensen’s inequality. □

## D.2 Proof of (12)

From (10) it follows that each of the inequalities below hold with probability at least 1 − 2δ over data

$$
\forall \rho \in \mathcal { M } _ { \pi } : E _ { \theta \sim \rho } \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) \leq E _ { \theta \sim \rho } \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) + \frac { L ( \ln ( N ) ) ^ { r } } { \sqrt { N } } ( \mathrm { K L } ( \rho | | \pi ) + \ln \frac { C _ { \delta } } { \delta } + \mathcal { C } ( \delta , 1 ) )\tag{131}
$$

$$
\forall \rho \in \mathcal { M } _ { \pi } : - E _ { \theta \sim \rho } \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) \leq - E _ { \theta \sim \rho } \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) + \frac { L ( \ln ( N ) ) ^ { r } } { \sqrt { N } } ( \mathrm { K L } ( \rho | | \pi ) + \ln \frac { C _ { \delta } } { \delta } + \mathcal { C } ( \delta , - 1 ) )\tag{132}
$$

In particular, by using the union bound it follows that (131) and (132) hold simultaneously with probability at least $1 - 4 \delta$ . Using the fact that for any posterior $\rho \in \mathcal { M } _ { \pi }$ ，

$$
E _ { \theta \sim \rho ^ { \mathrm { c i b s . } } } \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) + \frac { L ( \ln ( N ) ) ^ { r } } { \sqrt { N } } \mathrm { K L } ( \rho ^ { \mathrm { G i b s s } } | | \pi ) \le E _ { \theta \sim \rho } \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) + \frac { L ( \ln ( N ) ) ^ { r } } { \sqrt { N } } ( \mathrm { K L } ( \rho | | \pi )
$$

it then follows that with probability at least $1 - 4 \delta$ over data

## D.3 Proof of (13)

If (SConv) holds, then by [30, Theorem 2.1.11, Theorem 2.1.8]

$$
m _ { \Theta } \lvert | \theta _ { \star } - \theta _ { t r u e } \rvert | ^ { 2 } \leq \mathcal { L } ( \theta _ { \star } ) - \mathcal { L } ( \theta _ { t r u e } )
$$

from which using (12) the inequality (13) follows.

## D.4 Proof of (14)

From [26, Chapter 4, page 87] it follows that $\sigma _ { \mathbf { e } ^ { s } } ^ { 2 } = { \mathcal { L } } ( \theta _ { \mathrm { t r u e } } ) = { \mathrm { i n f } } _ { \theta \in \Theta } { \mathcal { L } } ( \theta )$ , where $\sigma _ { \mathbf { e } ^ { s } } ^ { 2 } ~ =$ $\mathbf { E } [ \| \mathbf { e } ^ { s } ( t ) \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } ]$ , if ℓ is the quadratic loss function, i.e. the true system yields the best possible prediction in mean square sense.

$$
\forall \theta \in \Theta : \sigma _ { \mathbf { e } ^ { s } } ^ { 2 } = \mathcal { L } ( \theta _ { t r u e } ) \leq \mathcal { L } ( \theta )\tag{142}
$$

where $\sigma _ { \mathbf { e } ^ { s } } ^ { 2 }$ is the variance of $\mathbf { e } ^ { s } ( t )$ . That is, the parameter $\theta _ { t r u e }$ corresponding to the data generator results in the smallest possible generalisation loss for quadratic loss function. Let $H _ { \theta }$ be the transfer function of the predictor parametrized by θ

It can be shown (see [12, Appendix, (82)]) and [26, Chapter 4, page 87]) that

$$
\| H _ { \theta _ { \mathrm { t r u e } } } - H _ { \theta } \| _ { H _ { 2 } } ^ { 2 } \leq \frac { 1 } { m _ { \mathbf { w } } } ( \mathcal { L } ( \theta ) - \mathcal { L } ( \theta _ { t r u e } ) ) ,\tag{143}
$$

Then (14) follows from (12) and (143).

## D.5 Remark on relationship with system identification and learning

Remark D.5 (Identification of FIR models). Consider the SISO case $( n _ { y } = n _ { u } = 1 )$ , fix an integer $T _ { 0 }$ and let $\Theta = \mathbb { R } ^ { T _ { 0 } }$ and for any $\boldsymbol { \theta } = ( \theta _ { 1 } , \ldots , \theta _ { T _ { 0 } } ) ^ { T } \in \Theta$ , let $D _ { \theta } = \theta _ { 1 }$

$$
A = \left[ \begin{array} { l l l l l } { 0 } & { 0 } & { \cdots } & { 0 } & { 0 } \\ { 1 } & { 0 } & { \cdots } & { 0 } & { 0 } \\ { \vdots } & { \vdots } & { \dots } & { \vdots } & { \vdots } \\ { 0 } & { 0 } & { \cdots } & { 1 } & { 0 } \end{array} \right] , B = \left[ \begin{array} { l } { 1 } \\ { 0 } \\ { \vdots } \\ { \vdots } \\ { 0 } \end{array} \right] , C _ { \theta } = \left[ \begin{array} { l } { \theta _ { 2 } } \\ { \vdots } \\ { \theta _ { T _ { 0 } } } \\ { 1 } \end{array} \right] ^ { T }
$$

Then $\begin{array} { r } { \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t ) = u ( t - T _ { 0 } ) + \sum _ { i = 0 } ^ { T _ { 0 } - 1 } \theta _ { i + 1 } \mathbf { u } ( t - i ) } \end{array}$ . Assume that there exists $\theta _ { t r u e } \in \Theta$ , such that $\mathbf { y } ( t ) = \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta _ { t r u e } } ( t ) + \mathbf { e } ( t )$ , where $\mathbf { e } ( t )$ is i.i.d. with zero mean and variance $\sigma ^ { 2 } { } _ { ; }$ , and independent $o f \mathbf { u } ( s ) , s \in \mathbb { Z } .$ Let $\hat { \mathbf { u } } ( t ) = \mathbf { u } ( t ) f o r \ d \mathbf { \Sigma } > 0$ and let $\hat { \mathbf { u } } ( t ) = 0 \mathrm { \ : } f o r \ : t < 0$ . Let Φ be the regressor matrix constructedfrom u˜ as in [34, (3.45), page 48] and let $\begin{array} { r } { Y = \left[ \mathbf { y } ( 0 ) \quad \cdots \quad \mathbf { y } ( N - 1 ) \right] ^ { T } } \end{array}$ . Then $\begin{array} { r } { \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) = \frac { 1 } { N } \| Y - \Phi \theta \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } . } \end{array}$ . Assume that the prior π is the Gaussian density $\mathcal { N } ( \tilde { \theta } , P ) , P = P ^ { T } > 0$ Then a lengthy but straightforward calculation reveal that the Gibbs posterior is the Gaussian density $\begin{array} { r l } { \small } & { { } \mathcal { N } ( \widetilde { \theta } + P \Phi ^ { T } ( \Phi P \Phi ^ { \dot { T } } + \frac { N } { 2 \lambda } I ) ^ { - 1 } ( Y - \Phi \widetilde { \theta } ) , P - P \Phi ^ { T } ( \Phi P \Phi ^ { T } + \frac { N } { 2 \lambda } I ) ^ { - 1 } \Phi P ) } \end{array}$ , which is the conditional density of θ w.r.t. Y, Φ presented in [34, (4.6), page $\bar { I } O I J , i f Y = \Phi \dot { \theta } + E$ is assumed with $\begin{array} { r } { E \sim \mathcal { N } ( 0 , \frac { N } { 2 \lambda } I _ { T _ { 0 } } ) } \end{array}$ . Then the MAP and MEP estimates are both $\theta _ { \star } = \widetilde { \theta } + P \Phi ^ { T } ( \Phi P \Phi ^ { T } + \frac { N } { 2 \lambda } I ) ^ { - 1 } ( Y -$ $\Phi \theta _ { t r u e } )$ and they solve the regularized optimization problem arg min $_ { \cdot \theta \in \Theta } \lambda \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) + ( \theta - \tilde { \theta } ) P ^ { - 1 } ( \theta - \tilde { \theta } )$ Moreover, they are also the maximum likelihood ofthe posterior $P ( \tilde { \theta } \mid Y , \Phi )$ .

Then Corollary 3.4 applies to $\theta _ { \star }$ , yielding a $O \big ( \frac { ( \ln ( N ) ) ^ { r } } { \sqrt { N } } \big )$ bound on the generalization gap of $\dag \theta _ { \star }$ and a $O \big ( \frac { ( \ln ( N ) ) ^ { r + 1 } } { \sqrt { N } } \big )$ bound on the square parameter estimation error $\lVert \theta _ { \star } - \theta _ { t r u e } \rVert _ { 2 } ^ { 2 }$

Remark D.6. (Maximum likelihood estimates of state-space models). Consider the parametrization ofLTI systems

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \hat { \mathbf { x } } ( t + 1 ) = ( \hat { A } _ { \theta } + \hat { L } _ { \theta } \hat { C } _ { \theta } ) \hat { \mathbf { x } } ( t ) + ( \hat { B } _ { \theta } + L _ { \theta } D _ { \theta } ) \mathbf { u } ( t ) + \hat { L } _ { \theta } \mathbf { e } _ { \theta } ( t ) , } \\ { \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) = \hat { C } _ { \theta } \hat { \mathbf { x } } ( t ) + \hat { D } _ { \theta } \mathbf { u } ( t ) + \mathbf { e } _ { \theta } ( t ) ~ } \end{array}\tag{144}
$$

where $A _ { \theta }$ and $A _ { \theta } + L _ { \theta } C _ { \theta }$ are Schur, $\mathbf { e } _ { \theta } ( t )$ $i . i . d . ,$ , zero mean Gaussian with variance $\textstyle { \frac { 1 } { \lambda } } , \left( \mathbf { u } , \mathbf { e } _ { \theta } \right)$ is jointly Gaussian, $\mathbf { e } _ { \theta } ( t )$ is independent of $\mathbf { \dot { u } } ( s ) f o r s < t .$ . We can associate with (144) the predictor

$$
\begin{array} { r l } & { \hat { \mathbf { x } } ( t + 1 ) = \hat { A } _ { \theta } \hat { \mathbf { x } } ( t ) + \hat { B } _ { \theta } \mathbf { u } ( t ) + \hat { L } _ { \theta } \mathbf { y } ( t ) , } \\ & { \hat { \mathbf { y } } _ { \theta } ( t \mid 0 ) = \hat { C } _ { \theta } \hat { \mathbf { x } } ( t ) + \hat { D } _ { \theta } \mathbf { u } ( t ) , } \end{array}\tag{145}
$$

Assume that y is generated by an LTI system of the form (144) which satisfies [4, (1), INP B, page 427].

In this case, by [4, (2.7), page 422, Chapter 7] the conditional likelihoodfunction $p _ { \theta } ( \{ y ( t ) \} _ { t = - 1 } ^ { N - 1 } \ |$ $\{ u ( t ) \} _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } ) \ \{ \mathbf { y } _ { \theta } ( t ) \} _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 }$ w.r.t $\{ \mathbf { u } ( t ) \} _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 }$ , evaluated at $y ( t ) \ = \ \mathbf { y } ( t )$ and $u ( t ) \ = \ \mathbf { u } ( t ) , \ t \ =$ $- 1 , \ldots , N - 1$ equals $Z e ^ { - \frac { N \lambda } { 2 } \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } \left( \theta \right) }$ for a suitable constant $Z ,$ where the empirical error $\hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta )$ is defined for the quadratic loss. That is, i.e., up to an additive constant, its logarithm equals ln $\dot { \rho } ^ { G i b b s } ( \theta )$ In particular, the maximum likelihood estimate argma $\mathrm { x } _ { \theta \in \Theta } p _ { \theta } \big ( \{ y ( t ) \} _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } ~ | ~ \{ u ( t ) \} _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 } \big )$ , see [4, Chapter 7,page 428] equals the MAP argma $\operatorname { v } _ { \theta \in \Theta } \rho ^ { G i b b s } ( \theta )$ ofthe Gibbs posterior corresponding to the uniform distribution $\begin{array} { r } { \pi = \frac { 1 } { v o l ( \Theta ) } } \end{array}$ as a prior. Here we assume that vol(Θ) isfinite.

We can then use the bounds (11), (12), (13), (14), for the generalization gap, the gap between the true error of the learned model and the best true error and the parameter estimation error respectively.

Remark D.7 (Bayesian methods for estimating LTI systems). The Bayesian method for system identification of LTI systems [31] corresponds to taking a single draw, MEP or MAP from the conditional distribution of the parameter given the past observations. If the parametrization is in innovation form, i.e., of the form (144) and all the signals are jointly Gaussian, then by Remark D.6 the joint density of $\{ \mathbf { y } ( t ) \} _ { t = - 1 } ^ { N - 1 }$ given $\{ \mathbf { u } ( t ) \} _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 }$ and $\{ \mathbf { y } ( s ) , \mathbf { u } ( s ) \} _ { s < 0 }$ and a parameter θ is $Z e ^ { - N \lambda \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) }$ for a suitable constant, and hence by [31, Section 3, eq. (12)] the conditional density of θ with respect to $\{ \mathbf { y } ( t ) , \mathbf { u } ( t ) \} _ { t = 0 } ^ { N - 1 }$ is the Gibbs posterior $\frac { e ^ { - N \lambda \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) } p _ { \theta } ( \mathbf { y } ( - 1 ) ) \pi ( \theta ) } { E _ { \theta \in \pi } e ^ { - \lambda \hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) } }$ for the quadratic loss and a suitable prior, and hence Gibbs posterior for the predictors (145), and hence taking a random draw, MEP or MAP from this conditional density, as suggested by [31], corresponds to taking a random draw, MEP or MAPfrom the Gibbs posterior. In particular, the bounds (11), (12), (13), (14) apply.

Remark D.8 (PEM methods). To begin with ifa PEM algorithm can be reduced to optimizing a cost function $\hat { \mathcal { L } } _ { N } ( \theta ) + g ( \theta )$ , then the algorithm is equivalent to taking the MAP estimate of the Gibbs posterior with a prior π such that $\begin{array} { r } { - \frac { 1 } { \lambda } \ln \pi ( \theta ) = \frac { g ( \theta ) } { \lambda } } \end{array}$ . In particular, (11)-(14) from Corollary 3.4 applies in this case. For example, the quadratic regularization term $g ( \theta ) = ( \theta - \theta _ { m } ) ^ { T } P ^ { - 1 } ( \theta - \theta _ { m } )$ corresponds to $\pi \sim \mathcal { N } ( \theta _ { m } , \hat { P } / \lambda )$ . If π can be interpreted as a Gaussian density, then MAP is MEP and the corresponding sharper bounds can be used.

Furthermore, many PEM algorithms rely on choosing randomly the initial parameter estimate. The outcomes of such algorithms can be viewed as drawing a sample from a suitable posterior distribution. More precisely, consoider an algorithm $A _ { N }$ . For every initial value $\theta _ { 0 } \in \Theta$ and data D, the algorithm generates an estimate $A _ { N } ( \theta _ { 0 } , { \mathcal { D } } )$ . Most optimization algorithms usedfor PEM methods (Gradient, Gauss-Newton), etc. are of this form, if we fix the number of steps. Assume that the initial parameter values $\theta _ { i n i t }$ are i.i.d., samples from a prior density π. Then the resulting parameter values $A _ { N } ( \theta _ { i n i t } , \mathcal { D } )$ are i.i.d. samples from a distribution $\begin{array} { r } { \dot { P _ { A _ { N } } } ( B ) = \int _ { A _ { N } ^ { - 1 } ( B , D ) } \pi ( \bar { \theta } ) d m ( \theta ) f o r } \end{array}$ any $B \in B _ { \theta }$ . Assume that the latter distribution has a density ρˆ which is in $\mathcal { M } _ { \pi }$ . Then Lemma D.3 provides an error boundfor the true error ofsuch an algorithm, when applied to an initial parameter value randomly sampled from π.

Remark D.9 (Strong convexity of the true error). Strong convexity implies that there is a unique minimizer ofthe true error, which is the true system. It can be seen as an identifiability and persistence of excitation assumption, as it implies that there is no parameter value which gives the same inputoutput behavior as the true system (otherwise their true errores would be the same), and w is rich enough (the average response to w allows us to find the true system). In fact, strong convexity of the true error (at least locally), is a standard assumptionfor studying the asympotic distribution ofthe parameter estimation error [26, Theorem 9.1].

Remark D.10 (Bounds on the difference between system matrices). Assuming that the LTI systems Σ(θ) are all minimal, we can use (14) and [32, Theorem V.2] to derive an error bound (in highprobability) on the difference between the matrices ofthe learned and true system. Alternatively, if we consider parametrizations based on structure indices [33, Chapter 3] we could use the fact that $H _ { 2 }$ norms can be used to approximate locally the Riemannian distance between parameter values [33, Chapter 4, page 129].

Remark D.11 (Relationship with [11, 8]). For the case of bounded noise in the data generator, Theorem 3.1 provides a PAC-Bayesian bound on the generalization gap which is similar to [11,

Theorem 2, Corollary 1] and [8, Theorem 5.2]. However, [11] considers RNNs, and the corresponding constants $L _ { \mathbf { v } } ( \theta ) , L _ { g , \mathbf { s } } ( \theta ) , L _ { g , \mathbf { v } }$ correspond to $\| \hat { B } _ { \theta } \| _ { 2 } , \| \hat { C } _ { \theta } \| _ { 2 } ,$ and $\| \hat { D } _ { \theta } \| _ { 2 } ,$ respectively, and $\tau ( \theta )$ corresponds to an upper bound on the spectrum ofA<sup>ˆ</sup> and $C ( \theta )$ is such that $\| \hat { A } _ { \theta } ^ { k } \| _ { 2 } \leq \tau ( \theta ) ^ { k } C ( \theta )$ In our setting, $L _ { \mathbf { v } } ( \theta ) , L _ { g , \mathbf { s } } ( \theta ) , L _ { g , \mathbf { v } }$ can be taken to be C from Assumption 2.1, C(θ) can be taken to be M and τ(θ) can be taken to be γ from Assumption 2.1. Then the constants G , H from [11] are upper bounds on $\| \alpha _ { \theta } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }$ and $\theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha _ { \theta } )$ respectively. Hence the term $\hat { \Psi } _ { \pi } ( \lambda , N )$ of[11, Theorem 2, Corollary 1] is an upper bound on the term $\textstyle { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { 2 }$ ln $E _ { \theta \sim \pi } \exp \left( \mathcal { C } _ { i } ( 2 \lambda , \theta , N , \delta , \epsilon ) \right)$ from (8), i.e., the bound of [11, Theorem 2, Corollary 1] is more conservative than the one of this paper.

As to [8, Theorem 5.2], it uses $\lVert \alpha _ { g , y } - c _ { \theta } \rVert _ { \ell _ { 1 } } \leq G _ { e } ( \theta )$ and $\theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha _ { g , y } - c _ { \theta } ) \le G _ { e , 1 } ( \theta )$ in the bounds, where c<sub>θ</sub> and $\alpha _ { g , y }$ are defined in Lemma B.3. While this may appear less conservative, the use ofc<sub>θ</sub> makes the bound difficult to evaluate in practice, as c depends on the parameters ofthe unknown data generator. In contrast, our bound uses only $\| \alpha _ { g } \| _ { \ell _ { 1 } }$ and $\theta _ { \infty } ( \alpha _ { g } )$ , which, although they also depend on the data generator, are easier to estimate or upper bound in practice.

Note that [11, 8] do not address the case ofunbounded signals or parameter estimation errors. They also do not provide generalization boundsfor single draw, MEP, or MAP estimators.

Remark D.12 (Relationship with finite-sample bounds [32, 24, 39, 42, 20]). Recent works such as [32, 24, 39, 42, 20] provide finite-sample parameter estimation bounds for specific algorithms (e.g., least-squaresfor ARX models, subspace methods), typically assuming the data generator startsfrom a deterministic initial state and often neglecting the stochastic component. However, assuming a deterministic initial state can be problematic, since process noise generally drives the system to a random initial state determined by past disturbances.

In contrast, our results: (1) apply to a broad class oflearning algorithms (including PEM, maximum likelihood, Bayesian, and regularized FIR methods), (2) assume the data generator is in a stationary regime, (3) explicitly account for both deterministic and stochastic components, and (4) provide generalization gap bounds. The trade-off is that our bound on the square of the parameter estimation error is $O \left( \frac { ( \ln N ) ^ { r + 1 } } { \sqrt { N } } \right)$ , which is more conservative than the $O \left( { \frac { \ln N } { N } } \right)$ rates in the cited works.
